<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove a uniform spectral gap for odd open spin-one antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chains with the same fixed magnetic field at both endpoints. The field $`h=3/5`$ gives a gap greater than $`\log(10)/392`$ on every chain of $`2L+1`$ sites with <i>L</i> ≥ 960. Tasaki's index theorem then gives index −1 for every subsequential local limit of these boundary-selected ground states.

- **claim-fixed-field-uniform-gap** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For the Hamiltonian <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>, with the spin normalization above, $$E_1(L,3/5)-E_0(L,3/5)>\frac{\log 10}{392} \qquad\text{for every integer }L\ge960.$$
  - Explanation: The odd open chain with the specified spin normalization and identical endpoint fields has a strictly positive excitation gap uniformly over the stated lengths.
- **claim-open-boundary-transfer** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tr-boundary)): For $b>0$, $n\ge2$, and $g\in\mathcal G$, the pairing $\langle v_b,X_b^nU_gv_b\rangle$ is the shifted partition function of the open chain with left field $-hS_0^z$ and right field $+hS_{n-1}^{ge_z}$. Consequently $$\label{tr:open} Y_n(b)=\langle v_b,X_b^nv_b\rangle,\qquad Y_n^{\rm s}(b)=\langle v_b,X_b^nU_Pv_b\rangle.$$ Here $S^u=\sum_\alpha u_\alpha S^\alpha$ for a real vector $u$.
  - Explanation: Boundary vectors and a rotation of the right vector represent the shifted partition functions with the corresponding physical endpoint fields.
- **claim-probability-squaring-concentration** (bound, [source](#stmt-per-purity)): Let $(w_i)$ be a finite or countable probability list, and suppose its purity is at least $1-u$, where $0\le u<1$. Normalizing the squares $w_i^2$ gives a list with purity defect at most $$\label{per:f} f(u)=\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)^2}.$$ If $u<1/2$, the largest weight is unique and at least $1-u$. Writing $x$ for the sum of all the other weights, one has $$\label{per:concentration-elementary} x\le\frac{1-\sqrt{1-2u}}2.$$ In particular, for $0\le u\le .1$, $$\label{per:d} \frac{x}{1-x}\le d(u),\qquad d(u)=\frac{u}{2-3u}.$$
  - Explanation: Squaring and renormalizing a probability list improves its purity defect. A sufficiently small original defect also forces a unique dominant weight and bounds the remaining mass.
- **claim-coupled-periodic-purity-update** (bound, [source](#stmt-per-coupled)): Suppose that $n\ge4$ is even, $b>0$, and $S(n,b)\ge s$, $T(2n,b)\ge t$, with $0<s,t\le1$. Set $$p_+=f(1-s^2t).$$ If $p_+<1$, then $$\label{per:coupled-formula} 1-S(2n,2b)\le p_+,\qquad 1-T(4n,2b)\le f\bigl(1-t^2(1-p_+)\bigr).$$ Either upper defect bound may be increased before subsequent use, provided it stays below one.
  - Explanation: Partition-function cancellation and probability squaring propagate spatial and physical purity bounds when length and inverse temperature double.
- **claim-finite-periodic-inputs** (bound, [source](#stmt-per-native)): Set $$\label{per:constants} \ell=.999,\quad U=1.00139,\quad V=.872,\quad m_J=1.0657205,\quad m_N=.2783155.$$ At $b_0=49/4$, the spatial eigenvalue list decomposes as one list $J$ and three identical lists $N$. Here $J$ is the spectrum on the subspace fixed by every $U_g$, $g\in D_2$, and $N$ is the common spectrum on each of the other three simultaneous eigenspaces of these commuting operators. In $J$ the absolute eigenvalues are at most $U$, and their twelfth powers sum to at most $m_J$; in each $N$ they are at most $V$, and their twelfth powers sum to at most $m_N$. There is an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\ell$. Furthermore, $$\label{per:native-first-row} 1-S(72,b_1)\le\frac{151249}{2500000}=.0604996.$$ For the periodic chains of $72$ and $120$ sites, the shifted ground energy is strictly negative. Consequently, at each of these lengths and every $b>0$, an individual unnormalized ground Boltzmann weight is greater than one.
  - Explanation: Finite periodic certificates supply sectorwise spectral bounds, a spatial-purity seed, and negative shifted ground energies at the designated periodic lengths.
- **claim-doubling-scale-spatial-concentration** (theorem, [source](#stmt-per-concentration)): For every $j\ge0$, $X_{b_j}$ has a unique simple eigenvalue of largest modulus. This eigenvalue is positive; denote it by $\lambda_j=\\|X_{b_j}\\|$. Its eigenspace is fixed pointwise by $\mathcal G$. Let $\rho_j$ be the supremum of the absolute values of all other eigenvalues divided by $\lambda_j$. Then $$\label{per:initial-ratios} \ell<\lambda_0\le U,\qquad \rho_0<.874,\qquad \rho_1<.9525,\qquad \rho_2<.971.$$ For $j\ge2$, define $$\label{per:scales} n_j=120\,2^{j-2},\qquad M_j=2n_j,\qquad u_2=.052,\qquad u_{j+1}=6u_j^2.$$ The following bounds hold for every $j\ge2$: $$\begin{aligned} 1-S(n_j,b_j)&\le u_j,& 1-T(M_j,b_j)&\le u_j, \label{per:tracked-purities}\\ \rho_j^{n_j}&\le d(u_j). &&\label{per:spectral-ratio} \end{aligned}$$ Finally, put $r_j=\lambda_{j+1}/\lambda_j^2$. Then $$\label{per:lambda-ratios} 0<r_j\le1\quad(j\ge0),\qquad r_j^{M_j}\ge(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1})\quad(j\ge2).$$
  - Explanation: At every doubling scale the leading spatial eigenvalue is positive and simple, its line is fixed by the rotation group, and quantitative tail and temperature-comparison bounds hold.
- **claim-boundary-temperature-transfer** (bound, [source](#stmt-be-recurrence)): Let $j\ge0$ and let $m>k\ge2$ be even. Suppose $e_k(j)<1$ and $0<q<1$ with $q\ge\rho_{j+1}^{m-k}$. Define, for $0\le e<1$ and $0<t\le1$, $$\label{be:F} F(q,e,t)=\frac{q}{1-q}\left(\frac{1}{(1-e)^2t}-1\right).$$ Then $$\label{be:step} e_m(j+1)\le F(q,e_k(j),A(m,j)).$$ In particular, an upper bound strictly below one on the right proves $c_{j+1}>0$ without presupposing it.
  - Explanation: Residual spectral contraction and physical purity transfer boundary concentration to the next temperature. A strict residual bound establishes the new overlap rather than assuming it.
- **claim-coupled-boundary-update** (bound, [source](#stmt-be-coupled)): Suppose $j\ge2$, $0\le E_j,\eta_j<1$, and $$e_{k_j}(j)\le E_j,\qquad A(M_j,j)\ge1-\eta_j.$$ Let $E_{j+1},\eta_{j+1}\in[0,1)$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned} E_{j+1}&\ge F\left(d(u_{j+1})^{(M_j-k_j)/M_j},E_j,1-\eta_j\right), \label{be:Eupdate}\\ \eta_{j+1}&\ge f\left(1-(1-\eta_j)(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1}) (1-E_{j+1})\right). \label{be:etaupdate} \end{aligned}$$ Then $e_{k_{j+1}}(j+1)\le E_{j+1}$ and $A(M_{j+1},j+1)\ge1-\eta_{j+1}$.
  - Explanation: Admissible upper bounds for the coupled update propagate both the boundary residual estimate and the opposite-field physical-purity estimate.
- **claim-finite-boundary-initialization** (bound, [source](#stmt-in-finite)): Let $\alpha=.4042$ and $P_*(x)=x^3-.14x^2-.18x$. For the opposite-field Hamiltonian <a href="#tr:Hopp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Hopp">[tr:Hopp]</a>, $$\label{in:trials} E_0(H_m^{\rm opp})+am<\alpha\qquad(m=34,78,142,240).$$ At inverse temperature $b_0$, $$\label{in:thermal} Y_6(b_0)<.027,\qquad c_0P_*(\lambda_0)^2<.003745.$$
  - Explanation: Opposite-field variational energies and short-chain thermal estimates provide the finite data needed to initialize boundary concentration.
- **claim-initial-boundary-residual-and-purity** (bound, [source](#stmt-in-seedbounds)): At these four lengths, $$\label{in:seedineq} e_m(0)\le\epsilon(m),\qquad A(m,j)\ge\bigl(1+R(m)^{2^j}\bigr)^{-2}\quad(j\ge0).$$
  - Explanation: The thermal residual estimate bounds the initial boundary defect, while a variational ground weight bounds physical purity at later inverse temperatures.
- **claim-common-quadratic-majorant** (bound, [source](#stmt-in-quadratic)): Define $x_5=.01$ and $x_{j+1}=10x_j^2$ for $j\ge5$. Then, for every $j\ge5$, $$\label{in:common} u_j\le x_j,\qquad e_{k_j}(j)\le x_j,\qquad 1-A(M_j,j)\le x_j.$$ In particular $$\label{in:xexplicit} x_j=10^{-1-2^{j-5}}.$$
  - Explanation: An explicit quadratic recurrence simultaneously bounds the spatial-purity defect, the boundary residual fraction, and the opposite-field physical-purity defect at every later scale.
- **claim-signed-same-field-comparison** (bound, [source](#stmt-co-signed)): For $j\ge0$, an even $M\ge2$, and any integer $n\ge M$, define $Q_j(n)=Y_n^{\rm s}(b_j)/\lambda_j^n$. Then $$\label{co:error} \|Q_j(n)-c_j\|\le B_M(j)-c_j.$$
  - Explanation: A concentrated even opposite-field moment controls the signed same-field pairing at all longer integer lengths, including odd lengths and negative residual spatial eigenvalues.
- **claim-same-field-purity-on-length-intervals** (bound, [source](#stmt-co-purity)): For $j\ge5$ and every integer $n$ with $M_j\le n\le2M_j$, $$\label{co:purity-bound} \frac{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_{j+1})}{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_j)^2}\ge1-4x_j.$$
  - Explanation: The same-field physical Gibbs purity is uniformly close to maximal throughout each stated interval of lengths.
- **claim-boundary-selected-local-gap-and-index** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-topology)): Every boundary-selected state <a href="#top:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:limit">[top:limit]</a> is invariant under uniform rotations about the $z$ axis and under reflection $j\mapsto-j$. With $\gamma_*=(\log10)/392$, it satisfies $$\label{top:local-gap} \omega\bigl(A^*[H_{\rm bulk},A]\bigr) \ge\gamma_*\bigl(\omega(A^*A)-\|\omega(A)\|^2\bigr) \qquad\text{for every local }A.$$ In particular it is a locally-unique gapped ground state in the sense of (Tasaki 2025, Definition 11) [[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)], and its Tasaki index is $-1$.
  - Explanation: Every boundary-selected subsequential local limit inherits rotation and reflection invariance, satisfies the bulk local gap inequality, and has the stated Tasaki index.
- **claim-certified-boundary-thermal-moments** (bound, [source](#stmt-appth-bounds)): Let $\lambda_0$ be the positive leading eigenvalue of $X_{b_0}$, and let $c_0$ be the squared norm of the projection of $v_{b_0}$ onto its eigenspace. For $$P_*(x)=x^3-.14x^2-.18x,$$ one has $$\label{appth:conclusions} Y_6(b_0)<.027, \qquad c_0P_*(\lambda_0)^2<.003745.$$
  - Explanation: Certified short-chain thermal traces bound both the residual seed and the polynomially filtered leading spatial contribution.
- **claim-certified-matrix-exponential-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-appth-matrix-error)): For every pair $(n,g)$ in <a href="#appth:integer-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:integer-table">[appth:integer-table]</a>, $$\left\\|\widehat E-\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+C_nI)/2]\right\\|_{\rm F} <2\cdot10^{-10}.$$
  - Explanation: The integer Horner approximation has a rigorous Frobenius error bound for every short-chain length and endpoint orientation in the thermal table.
- **claim-exact-opposite-field-variational-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-apptr-bound)): Let $E_0^{\mathrm{opp}}(m)$ be the smallest eigenvalue of $H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}$. Then $$\label{apptr:four-bounds} E_0^{\mathrm{opp}}(m)+am<\alpha \qquad\text{for }m\in\{34,78,142,240\}.$$
  - Explanation: Explicit nonzero matrix-product trial vectors certify strict upper bounds on the shifted opposite-field ground energy at the designated lengths.
- **claim-integer-matrix-product-contractions** (construction, [source](#stmt-apptr-contractions)): With the data above, the norm $V_m=\langle\psi_m,\psi_m\rangle$ and the scaled shifted energy numerator $$H_m^*=500000\, \langle\psi_m,(H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}+amI)\psi_m\rangle$$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned} V_m&=L_mu,\label{apptr:norm}\\ H_m^*&=500000\sum_{r=0}^{m-2} L_0T_*^rO_*T_*^{m-2-r}u -300000L_0G_*T_*^{m-1}u\notag\\ &\quad+300000L_{m-1}G_*u+700741mL_mu. \label{apptr:energy-expanded} \end{aligned}$$ In particular, $V_m$ and $H_m^*$ are integers.
  - Explanation: Exact transfer and insertion contractions evaluate the trial-vector norm and scaled shifted energy numerator as integers with the specified endpoint signs.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Spin normalization** ([source](#eq:target)): In the orthonormal basis $e_m$, $m=-1,0,1$, of $\mathbb C^3$, let $$S^ze_m=me_m,\qquad S^+e_m=\sqrt{2-m(m+1)}\,e_{m+1}\quad(m<1),\qquad S^+e_1=0,$$ and put $S^-=(S^+)^*$, $S^x=(S^++S^-)/2$, and $S^y=(S^+-S^-)/(2i)$.
- **Target odd open-chain Hamiltonian** ([source](#eq:target)): For $L\ge1$ and $h>0$, consider the operator on $(\mathbb C^3)^{\otimes(2L+1)}$ given by $$\label{eq:target} H_L(h)=\sum_{j=-L}^{L-1}\sum_{\alpha=x,y,z}S_j^\alpha S_{j+1}^\alpha -h(S_{-L}^z+S_L^z).$$
- **Shifted partition functions** ([source](#tr:constants)): Our partition functions are ordinary traces over the entire physical Hilbert space: $$\label{tr:partitions} \begin{split} Z_n(b)&=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm per}+anI)},\\ Y_n(b)&=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm opp}+anI)},\qquad Y_n^{\rm s}(b)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm s}+anI)}. \end{split}$$
- **Continuous bond history** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A history is an ordered list $\omega=((t_1,\alpha_1),\ldots,(t_d,\alpha_d))$ with $0<t_1<\cdots<t_d<b$ and $\alpha_r\in\{x,y,z\}$; the empty list is allowed.
- **Spatial transfer operator** ([source](#pm-h-8)): On $\mathcal K_b=L^2(\Omega_b,\mu_b;\mathbb C)$ define $$\label{tr:operator} (X_bf)(\omega)=e^{-ab}\int_{\Omega_b}k_b(\omega,\eta) f(\eta)\,d\mu_b(\eta).$$
- **Boundary history vector** ([source](#tr:vector)): Define $v_b\in\mathcal K_b$ by $$\label{tr:vector} v_b(\omega)= \begin{cases} (ih)^d,&\text{all $d$ marks of $\omega$ equal $z$},\\ 0,&\text{otherwise}. \end{cases}$$ The empty-history value is one. The simplex volumes give $\lVert v_b\rVert^2=\sum_{d\ge0}b^dh^{2d}/d!=e^{bh^2}$. The inner product below is conjugate linear in its first argument.
- **Spatial and physical periodic purities** ([source](#per:purities)): For even $n\ge4$, put $$\label{per:purities} S(n,b)=\frac{Z_{2n}(b)}{Z_n(b)^2},\qquad T(n,b)=\frac{Z_n(2b)}{Z_n(b)^2}.$$
- **Boundary overlap, moments, residual fraction, and physical purity** ([source](#be:definitions)): Let $P_j$ be the orthogonal projection onto its line, and put $$\label{be:definitions} \begin{split} c_j&=\lVert P_jv_{b_j}\rVert^2,\\ B_k(j)&=\langle v_{b_j},(\|X_{b_j}\|/\lambda_j)^k v_{b_j}\rangle, \qquad e_k(j)=\frac{B_k(j)-c_j}{B_k(j)},\\ A(m,j)&=\frac{Y_m(b_{j+1})}{Y_m(b_j)^2}. \end{split}$$
- **Boundary transfer update function** ([source](#stmt-be-recurrence)): Define, for $0\le e<1$ and $0<t\le1$, $$\label{be:F} F(q,e,t)=\frac{q}{1-q}\left(\frac{1}{(1-e)^2t}-1\right).$$
- **Local observable** ([source](#top:limit)): A local observable is a matrix acting on finitely many sites of $\mathbb Z$, extended by the identity on the other sites.
- **State on local observables** ([source](#top:limit)): A state on these observables is a normalized positive linear functional.
- **Boundary-selected state** ([source](#top:limit)): We call any such state *boundary-selected*.
- **Local twist** ([source](#top:twist)): To specify the index, for $\varepsilon>0$ define the local twist $$\label{top:twist} U_\varepsilon= \exp\left[-i\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z\cap[-\pi/\varepsilon,\pi/\varepsilon]} (\pi+\varepsilon j)S_j^z\right].$$
- **Open-boundary matrix-product trial vector** ([source](#apptr:physical-vector)): For each $m\ge2$, the physical vector is $$\label{apptr:physical-vector} \psi_m=\sum_{s_1,\ldots,s_m=-1}^{1} \bigl(\mathbf b^{\mathsf t}A_{s_1}\cdots A_{s_m}\mathbf c\bigr) e_{s_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{s_m}.$$
- **Invariant equal-weight contraction subspace** ([source](#apptr:restricted-labels)): Let $\mathcal K_0$ be the coordinate subspace with paired labels $$\label{apptr:restricted-labels} \mathcal Q=\{((i,d),(j,d)):(i,d),(j,d)\in\mathcal V\}.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-open-chain-transfer** [claim](#stmt-tr-boundary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tr-boundary): Expand the negative Hamiltonian exponential in ordered time, regroup histories by bonds and boundaries, and justify the regrouping by absolute convergence. -> Exact opposite-field and same-field boundary pairings.
- **step-probability-squaring** [claim](#stmt-per-purity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-per-purity): Compare the purity after squaring with the original pairwise mass, then isolate the largest probability and its complement. -> The squaring-defect bound and dominant-weight estimates.
- **step-periodic-coupled-update** [claim](#stmt-per-coupled) / [proof](#proof-stmt-per-coupled): Cancel partition functions to relate spatial and physical purities, then apply probability squaring in each direction. -> Coupled periodic defect bounds at doubled length and inverse temperature.
- **step-import-periodic-finite-inputs** [claim](#stmt-per-native) / [proof](#stmt-per-native): Use the explicitly stated companion results with the same shift, spin normalization, and bilinear coupling. -> The finite periodic information required by spatial concentration.
- **step-spatial-concentration** [claim](#stmt-per-concentration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-per-concentration): Separate the dominant initial spectral mass, reseed the coupled purity recurrence, propagate it inductively, and use positive odd partition functions and group actions to determine the leading sign and symmetry. -> A simple positive rotation-invariant leading line, residual-ratio bounds, and comparisons between successive leading eigenvalues.
- **step-boundary-temperature-transfer** [claim](#stmt-be-recurrence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-be-recurrence): Contract residual moments at the new temperature and cancel physical partition functions to bound their ratio using only the old overlap. -> A next-temperature boundary defect bound that can establish a positive new overlap.
- **step-coupled-boundary-update** [claim](#stmt-be-coupled) / [proof](#proof-stmt-be-coupled): Combine temperature transfer with physical partition-function cancellation, the lower leading-eigenvalue comparison, and Gibbs-probability squaring. -> Joint boundary residual and opposite-field purity control at the next scale.
- **step-thermal-matrix-error** [claim](#stmt-appth-matrix-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-appth-matrix-error): Bound the omitted Poisson mass, coefficient rounding, and entrywise Horner floors after undoing the integer similarity, propagating errors through the self-adjoint contraction. -> A rigorous Frobenius enclosure of the half-temperature exponential.
- **step-thermal-moment-certificates** [claim](#stmt-appth-bounds) / [proof](#appth:moment-centers): Convert the matrix error to partition-function intervals, enclose scalar exponentials rationally, average endpoint orientations, and evaluate the polynomial-filtered moment bound. -> Certified upper bounds for the short opposite-field trace and filtered leading contribution.
- **step-integer-trial-contractions** [claim](#stmt-apptr-contractions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-apptr-contractions): Expand products of real amplitudes in the orthonormal physical basis and insert the exact spin weights, bond matrix elements, endpoint signs, and per-site shift. -> Exact integer formulas for the trial norm and scaled shifted energy numerator.
- **step-variational-energy-certificates** [claim](#stmt-apptr-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-apptr-contractions-2): Restrict contractions to the invariant equal-weight subspace, run the exact row recurrence, verify positive norms and strict integer margins, and apply the variational principle. -> The strict opposite-field shifted ground-energy bounds.
- **step-assemble-finite-boundary-data** [claim](#stmt-in-finite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-finite): Assemble the independently specified thermal and variational certificates into the finite boundary inputs. -> The finite data used by the boundary initialization.
- **step-initial-boundary-seeds** [claim](#stmt-in-seedbounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-seedbounds): Contract the initial thermal tail, bound the filtered leading term using polynomial monotonicity, and compare all remaining Gibbs weights with a selected variationally bounded ground weight. -> Initial boundary defects and physical-purity bounds at the required finite lengths.
- **step-quadratic-continuation** [claim](#stmt-in-quadratic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-quadratic): Close the finite initialization table, enlarge the defects to a common seed, and bound both coupled outputs by the same quadratic majorant. -> Explicit defect decay at every later doubling scale.
- **step-signed-same-field-control** [claim](#stmt-co-signed) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-signed): Separate the fixed positive leading line and bound every residual rotated spectral pairing by Cauchy–Schwarz and an even absolute moment. -> Uniform signed-pairing control at all longer integer lengths.
- **step-same-field-purity** [claim](#stmt-co-purity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-purity): Use a common even reference length at adjacent temperatures, compare the leading-eigenvalue powers over the length interval, and bound the defect of the resulting product. -> A same-field Gibbs-purity lower bound throughout each dyadic length interval.
- **step-extract-uniform-physical-gap** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-purity-2): Bound the leading Gibbs probability by purity, bound the excited-to-ground weight ratio by the remaining mass, take logarithms, and cover all required odd lengths with the doubling intervals. -> The strict uniform finite-volume spectral gap for the target endpoint field.
- **step-boundary-selected-topological-index** [claim](#stmt-cor-topology) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-topology): Pass finite-volume symmetry and spectral inequalities to subsequential local limits, then apply the specified twist-index theorem with the thermodynamic limit preceding the twist limit. -> The local bulk gap inequality and Tasaki index for every boundary-selected state.

Consequently $$\\label{tr:open}\n Y_n(b)=\\langle v_b,X_b^nv_b\\rangle,\\qquad\n Y_n^{\\rm s}(b)=\\langle v_b,X_b^nU_Pv_b\\rangle.$$ Here $S^u=\\sum_\\alpha u_\\alpha S^\\alpha$ for a real vector $u$.",

Normalizing the squares $w_i^2$ gives a list with purity defect at most $$\\label{per:f}\n f(u)=\\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)^2}.$$ If $u<1/2$, the largest weight is unique and at least $1-u$. Writing $x$ for the sum of all the other weights, one has $$\\label{per:concentration-elementary}\n x\\le\\frac{1-\\sqrt{1-2u}}2.$$ In particular, for $0\\le u\\le .1$, $$\\label{per:d}\n \\frac{x}{1-x}\\le d(u),\\qquad d(u)=\\frac{u}{2-3u}.$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-fixed-boundary-gap**: The uniform physical gap concerns odd open chains with the specified spin normalization and identical endpoint fields fixed at 3/5, for half-chain lengths at least 960.
- **invariant-boundary-pairing-distinction**: Rotating the right auxiliary boundary vector changes the right physical field. The opposite-field and same-field partition functions require their respective boundary pairings.
- **invariant-integer-thermal-certificate**: The thermal approximation uses simultaneous integer Horner updates with floors toward negative infinity. The printed integer quotients provide finite targets for checking the recurrence.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-change-endpoint-model**: Apply the same quantitative gap to free endpoints, unequal fields or a different field strength. -> The theorem establishes its quantitative bound only for the specified identical endpoint fields.
- **anti-use-periodic-overlap-shortcut**: Infer concentration of the open-chain boundary pairing directly from concentration of periodic spatial traces. -> Boundary moments carry temperature-dependent overlap weights that periodic traces do not control.
- **anti-use-global-state-uniqueness**: Treat the index conclusion as a proof that the entire sequence of finite-volume states converges to a unique infinite-volume state. -> The conclusion is formulated for subsequential local limits.
- **anti-use-rounded-thermal-arithmetic**: Replace the integer recurrence with floating-point updates or truncation toward zero. -> The resulting quotient need not be the printed exact integer certificate, and the stated rounding analysis no longer applies.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The short-chain thermal appendix gives an explicit finite integer recurrence and printed integer quotients. This harness checks its entries at lengths 2 and 3 for every printed field orientation. It does not certify the full spectral-gap proof or the infinite-volume index.

```python
# INPUTS: A JSON object with integer n and g.
# PRECONDITIONS: n is 2 or 3; g is 1, -1 or 0; no additional fields.
# OUTPUTS: The exact integer floor of the scaled weighted Frobenius readout.
# TIME: Finite integer Horner evaluation on the declared short-chain domain.
# SPACE: Two dense integer arrays and a sparse integer row description.
# ARITHMETIC: Arbitrary-precision integers; division floors toward negative infinity.
# FAILURE: Invalid inputs raise an exception and produce no certificate.
import itertools
import json
import math
import sys

request = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(request, dict) or set(request) != {'n', 'g'}:
    raise ValueError('invalid input fields')
n, g = request['n'], request['g']
if type(n) is not int or type(g) is not int or n not in (2, 3) or g not in (1, -1, 0):
    raise ValueError('outside checked domain')
words = list(itertools.product((-1, 0, 1), repeat=n))
index = {word: row for row, word in enumerate(words)}
dimension = len(words)
parity = [sum(word) % 2 for word in words]
c10 = 30 * ((n - 1) // 2) + 20 * ((n - 1) % 2) + 12
rows = []
for row, word in enumerate(words):
    diagonal = 80 - c10 - 10 * sum(word[j] * word[j + 1] for j in range(n - 1)) + 6 * word[0] - 6 * g * word[-1]
    entries = [(row, diagonal)]
    for j in range(n - 1):
        for sign in (-1, 1):
            neighbor = list(word)
            neighbor[j] += sign
            neighbor[j + 1] -= sign
            neighbor = tuple(neighbor)
            if neighbor in index:
                entries.append((index[neighbor], -10))
    if g == 0:
        coefficient = -3 * (1 + parity[row])
        for sign in (-1, 1):
            neighbor = list(word)
            neighbor[-1] += sign
            neighbor = tuple(neighbor)
            if neighbor in index:
                entries.append((index[neighbor], coefficient))
    rows.append(entries)
K = 192
Q = 2 ** 50
factorial_K = math.factorial(K)
s = [49 ** i * factorial_K // math.factorial(i) for i in range(K + 1)]
total = sum(s)
t = [Q * coefficient // total for coefficient in s]
current = [[0] * dimension for _ in words]
for stage in range(K, -1, -1):
    updated = [[0] * dimension for _ in words]
    for row, entries in enumerate(rows):
        for col in range(dimension):
            value = sum(coefficient * current[neighbor][col] for neighbor, coefficient in entries)
            updated[row][col] = value // 80
        updated[row][row] += t[stage]
    current = updated
numerator = 0
for row in range(dimension):
    for col in range(dimension):
        weight = (2 - parity[row]) * (1 + parity[col])
        numerator += current[row][col] ** 2 * weight
answer = 10 ** 16 * numerator // (2 * Q ** 2)
print(answer)
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The finite variational certificate has a fully specified exact integer implementation: construct the supplied tensors, contract the norm and shifted energy, and check the strict variational margin. |
| games_simulation | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | For equilibrium quantum spin simulation, the finite thermal construction evaluates enclosed partition functions for the specified short chains and endpoint orientations. It supplies certified thermal quantities rather than a real-time evolution method. |
| tools | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | A certificate checker can reconstruct the finite trial calculation and verify the published integer comparisons without relying on a spectral approximation. |
| algorithms | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The paper gives finite recurrences for thermal matrix approximation and variational tensor contraction. Both have explicit initialization, update rules, and readouts suitable for direct implementation. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The paired virtual labels admit an exact restricted matrix representation because the transfer and insertion operators preserve virtual weight difference and the endpoints occupy the retained subspace. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The concrete computation is an exact integer contraction. The paper provides no accelerator architecture, finite-word arithmetic guarantee, or device implementation contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The rotation group and boundary symmetries control a spatial eigenspace and physical partition functions; they do not construct a cryptographic primitive or establish a security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The trial tensors and endpoints are fixed integer data used to certify energies. No training objective, learning procedure, or generalisation guarantee is supplied. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The finite variational construction supplies feasible trial vectors and exact objective upper-bound certificates for the specified ground-energy minimisation problems. It verifies supplied candidates without providing a general optimisation procedure. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The coupled updates relate chain length, inverse temperature, and purity defects. They do not specify communication, replicated state, or a distributed execution protocol. |
| other (Certified computational quantum spin physics) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The supplied finite thermal enclosures and matrix-product trial vectors construct reproducible certificates for boundary initialisation in the specified quantum spin model. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-target-spin-and-exchange** (formal_scope): The quantitative gap is specific to the stated spin normalization and pure bilinear interaction. Altering the local spin representation, exchange anisotropy, or interaction requires a separate argument. Blocked use: ['Transfer the numerical gap bound unchanged to higher-spin, anisotropic, or biquadratic Hamiltonians.']
- **limit-fixed-identical-endpoint-fields** (hypothesis): The main gap bound fixes identical endpoint fields at h=3/5. The opposite-field Hamiltonian supplies intermediate estimates rather than the same final gap assertion. Blocked use: ['Apply the main quantitative gap to absent, unequal, reversed, or differently sized endpoint fields.', 'Treat the opposite-field initialization as a uniform opposite-field gap theorem.']
- **limit-open-odd-chain-and-length** (formal_scope): The main theorem concerns odd open chains with L≥960 and no bond joining the endpoints. It does not settle the same quantitative bound for smaller chains, even open chains, or periodic closure. Blocked use: ['Claim the stated bound at every finite length.', 'Add a closing bond while retaining the open-chain theorem.']
- **limit-boundary-pairing-is-not-unitary-equivalence** (formal_scope): Rotating the auxiliary right boundary vector changes the physical right field in the transfer representation. This identity does not establish unitary equivalence of the physical same-field and opposite-field Hamiltonians. Blocked use: ['Identify the spectra of the physical same-field and opposite-field chains from the boundary rotation identity.']
- **limit-even-moment-positivity** (hypothesis): Positive absolute spatial moments are used at even reference lengths. Odd same-field powers retain spatial eigenvalue signs and require the separate signed comparison. Blocked use: ['Replace signed odd powers by absolute powers without an error estimate.', 'Apply the positive-moment boundary recurrence directly at odd reference lengths.']
- **limit-periodic-concentration-needs-boundary-overlap** (hypothesis): Concentration of periodic spatial traces leaves a boundary-overlap problem because boundary contributions have temperature-dependent weights. The boundary residual and physical purity must be controlled together. Blocked use: ['Deduce open-chain boundary concentration from the periodic gap alone.', 'Assume a positive new-temperature overlap before closing the boundary recurrence.']
- **limit-admissible-boundary-update** (hypothesis): Temperature transfer requires the old boundary defect to be strictly below one and the contraction factor to be strictly between zero and one while bounding the residual spectral power. The coupled update also requires admissible defect bounds and both displayed update inequalities. Blocked use: ['Divide by a vanishing old leading overlap.', 'Continue the coupled induction after an update bound reaches or exceeds one.', 'Replace either coupled inequality by an unchecked numerical estimate.']
- **limit-periodic-input-compatibility** (hypothesis): The periodic inputs must use the same spatial operator, shift, spin normalization, and unit bilinear coupling. The boundary proof needs intermediate spatial information rather than only a periodic gap conclusion. Blocked use: ['Substitute a periodic gap theorem without its required spatial spectral and finite purity inputs.', 'Mix periodic certificates from a differently normalized Hamiltonian.']
- **limit-exact-thermal-rounding** (approximation): The thermal recurrence uses exact integer operations, simultaneous updates, and entrywise floors toward negative infinity. Changing those conventions changes the approximation and invalidates its stated rounding analysis unless a new enclosure is proved. Blocked use: ['Use truncation toward zero for negative recurrence entries.', 'Update the thermal array in place.', 'Treat floating-point agreement with displayed digits as an exact recurrence certificate.']
- **limit-thermal-error-and-table-scope** (approximation): The matrix approximation error is proved for the pairs in the finite thermal table. Integer quotient agreement alone does not certify the exact thermal trace; the matrix error and scalar exponential enclosure must also be propagated. Blocked use: ['Extend the stated matrix error unchanged to larger chains or different endpoint orientations.', 'Equate the polynomial approximation with the exact exponential.', 'Infer the uniform physical gap from agreement with only some thermal table entries.']
- **limit-integer-similarity-not-contraction** (approximation): Error propagation uses the self-adjoint contraction after undoing the similarity. The generally nonsymmetric integer matrix is not asserted to be a contraction. Blocked use: ['Propagate approximation errors using an unproved contraction bound for the integer-similar matrix.']
- **limit-invariant-sector-restriction** (hypothesis): The restricted trial contraction is valid because every relevant operator preserves virtual-weight difference and both endpoint tensors lie in the retained equal-weight subspace. Endpoint support alone is insufficient to justify discarding intermediate labels. Blocked use: ['Prune virtual states solely because they have zero endpoint coefficients.', 'Take powers of a restricted matrix without establishing invariance of the retained subspace.']
- **limit-trial-energy-is-upper-bound** (formal_scope): The finite matrix-product construction provides nonzero trial vectors and variational upper bounds at the designated lengths. It neither computes the exact ground energy nor supplies a spectral gap by itself. Blocked use: ['Report the trial Rayleigh quotient as the exact ground energy.', 'Use a trial energy upper bound as a ground-energy lower bound or an excitation-gap certificate.', 'Infer a general ground-state optimization or convergence algorithm from the fixed trial construction.']
- **limit-trial-contraction-normalization-and-signs** (hypothesis): The exact variational contractions use ordinary real amplitudes in the orthonormal physical basis, the supplied integer tensors, the specified endpoint signs, and the per-site shift. The norm must be positive before forming a Rayleigh quotient. Blocked use: ['Insert an additional virtual metric or physical basis weight.', 'Reuse the energy recurrence after changing the right endpoint sign.', 'Accept an energy quotient without verifying a positive trial norm.']
- **limit-selected-state-existence-not-global-uniqueness** (nonconstructive): Boundary-selected infinite-volume states are obtained through compactness and a diagonal subsequence. The corollary does not prove convergence of the full finite-volume sequence or identify the selected states with every infinite-chain ground state. Blocked use: ['Claim a unique infinite-volume ground state from finite-volume simplicity.', 'Claim convergence of the entire sequence of ground-state expectations.', 'Apply a conclusion requiring additional global uniqueness.']
- **limit-local-gap-and-twist-order** (formal_scope): The infinite-volume local gap has a non-strict lower bound. The topological conclusion uses the specified boundary-selected states, their axial rotation and site-reflection symmetries, and the order in which the local state limit precedes the vanishing-twist limit. Blocked use: ['Transfer the strict finite-volume inequality unchanged into the limiting local gap statement.', 'Exchange the thermodynamic and twist limits without justification.', 'Identify the Tasaki twist index with every other topological diagnostic discussed in the introduction.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove a uniform spectral gap for odd open spin-one antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chains with the same fixed magnetic field at both endpoints. The field $`h=3/5`$ gives a gap greater than $`\log(10)/392`$ on every chain of $`2L+1`$ sites with <i>L</i> ≥ 960. Tasaki's index theorem then gives index −1 for every subsequential local limit of these boundary-selected ground states.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

Equal positive magnetic fields at the endpoints of an odd open spin-one Heisenberg chain select a unique finite-volume ground state (Tasaki 2025, Lemma 1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)]. The remaining question is whether the excitation gap stays positive as the chain grows. We prove a quantitative gap for one fixed field in the pure bilinear model. This is the boundary formulation used by Tasaki to connect the Haldane gap problem to a nontrivial topological index (Tasaki 2025) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)].

<a id="eq:target"></a>
In the orthonormal basis $e_m$, $m=-1,0,1$, of $\mathbb C^3$, let $$S^ze_m=me_m,\qquad
 S^+e_m=\sqrt{2-m(m+1)}\,e_{m+1}\quad(m<1),\qquad S^+e_1=0,$$ and put $S^-=(S^+)^*$, $S^x=(S^++S^-)/2$, and $S^y=(S^+-S^-)/(2i)$. Thus $\sum_\alpha(S^\alpha)^2=2I$. For $L\ge1$ and $h>0$, consider the operator on $(\mathbb C^3)^{\otimes(2L+1)}$ given by $$\label{eq:target}
 H_L(h)=\sum_{j=-L}^{L-1}\sum_{\alpha=x,y,z}S_j^\alpha S_{j+1}^\alpha
       -h(S_{-L}^z+S_L^z).$$ There is no bond between the endpoints. Write $E_0(L,h)\le E_1(L,h)$ for its two lowest eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity. We address the fixed-boundary-field gap assertion: for some single $h>0$, the difference $E_1(L,h)-E_0(L,h)$ stays bounded away from zero as $L\to\infty$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For the Hamiltonian <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>, with the spin normalization above, $$E_1(L,3/5)-E_0(L,3/5)>\frac{\log 10}{392}
 \qquad\text{for every integer }L\ge960.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Context and prior work.

Haldane’s prediction, formulated in 1981 and published in 1983, distinguishes integer and half-odd-integer isotropic antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chains, with a positive bulk gap for positive integer spin (Haldane 1981, 1983a, 1983b) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Haldane1981](#ref-Haldane1981), [Haldane1983PLA](#ref-Haldane1983PLA), [Haldane1983PRL](#ref-Haldane1983PRL)]. For spin one-half, Lieb, Schultz and Mattis had already constructed excitations of the periodic chain whose energy above the ground state vanishes with its length (Lieb, Schultz, and Mattis 1961, Appendix B) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[LSM1961](#ref-LSM1961)]. The Affleck–Kennedy–Lieb–Tasaki construction gave a rigorous gapped spin-one model (Affleck et al. 1987, 1988) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[AKLT1987](#ref-AKLT1987), [AKLT1988](#ref-AKLT1988)]. Its interaction contains a biquadratic term: with $q=\mathbf S_j\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1}$, it is proportional to $q+q^2/3$ up to an additive constant. Our Hamiltonian has the pure bilinear interaction $q$ and fixed endpoint fields.

The endpoint condition matters even when the bulk interaction is fixed. White and Huse numerically characterized the effective spin-one-half degrees of freedom at free ends (White and Huse 1993) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[WhiteHuse1993](#ref-WhiteHuse1993)]. To estimate bulk properties, including the gap, they added physical spin-one-half endpoint sites and adjusted their couplings. Magnetic fields on the original spin-one endpoints give a different finite-volume model, whose gap requires its own boundary estimates.

Tasaki formulated precisely this boundary problem in his work on the topological character of the spin-one Heisenberg ground state (Tasaki 2025) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)]. Equation (2) of that paper is the Hamiltonian <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>; Lemma 1 proves uniqueness for every $L\ge1$ and $h>0$. Assumption 2 asks for a positive gap uniform in all sufficiently large $L$, at one fixed $h>0$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves this fixed-boundary-field Haldane gap assertion positively and establishes that assumption. Section <a href="#sec:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:topology">7</a> then derives Tasaki index $-1$ for every boundary-selected subsequential local limit.

The topological viewpoint developed through several distinct descriptions. Den Nijs and Rommelse introduced nonlocal string order (Nijs and Rommelse 1989) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DenNijsRommelse1989](#ref-DenNijsRommelse1989)], and Kennedy and Tasaki connected it to hidden symmetry breaking (Kennedy and Tasaki 1992) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KennedyTasaki1992](#ref-KennedyTasaki1992)]. Pollmann, Turner, Berg and Oshikawa described symmetry protection through matrix-product states and the entanglement spectrum (Pollmann et al. 2010) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Pollmann2010](#ref-Pollmann2010)]; Ogata extended the bond-centered reflection index to infinite-chain states beyond the matrix-product description (Ogata 2021) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Ogata2021](#ref-Ogata2021)]. Tasaki’s local-twist index (Tasaki 2018) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Tasaki2018](#ref-Tasaki2018)] instead uses the twist expectation studied by Nakamura and Todo (Nakamura and Todo 2002) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[NakamuraTodo2002](#ref-NakamuraTodo2002)]. The boundary-state theorem in (Tasaki 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)] uses uniform $z$ rotations and site-centered reflection. These invariances imply invariance under site reflection combined with a global $\pi$ rotation about $z$, the symmetry studied by Fuji, Pollmann and Oshikawa (Fuji, Pollmann, and Oshikawa 2015) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[FujiPollmannOshikawa2015](#ref-FujiPollmannOshikawa2015)]. Our consequence concerns Tasaki’s index in this precise symmetry setting; it does not identify the different diagnostics above.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Methodological inputs.

For frustration-free chains, Lemm and Mozgunov proved finite-size criteria involving both interior and boundary-segment gaps (Lemm and Mozgunov 2019) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[LemmMozgunov2019](#ref-LemmMozgunov2019)]. Here the boundary information comes from weighted spatial moments and a coupled Gibbs-purity recurrence. The finite trial vectors use the matrix-product framework of finitely correlated states (Fannes, Nachtergaele, and Werner 1992) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[FNW1992](#ref-FNW1992)] and its finite-chain formulation (Pérez-García et al. 2007) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[PerezGarcia2007](#ref-PerezGarcia2007)]; they certify energy upper bounds that initialize the recurrence.

The continuous-history representation is an instance of the time-ordered Poisson expansion of Aizenman and Nachtergaele (Aizenman and Nachtergaele 1994) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[AizenmanNachtergaele1994](#ref-AizenmanNachtergaele1994)]. The particular spatial transfer operator and finite periodic estimates used here come from (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)], which proves a uniform gap on even periodic rings. We retain that source’s spin normalization, coupling, and energy shift, and state explicitly the transfer and finite-input results used below. The endpoint pairings and all estimates specific to the open chain are proved here.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Proof strategy.

For each inverse temperature $b>0$, a partition function of the periodic model is a trace of a power of a compact self-adjoint spatial operator $X_b$. For the open chain with *opposite* endpoint fields, the corresponding partition function is instead a boundary pairing $\langle v_b,X_b^n v_b\rangle$, where $n$ is the number of sites. At even $n$, this is a positive weighted moment of the absolute spatial eigenvalues. A rotation of the right boundary vector produces the same-field Hamiltonian in <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>.

The periodic estimates show that one spatial eigenvalue dominates sufficiently high even moments. For the open chain, however, the contribution of each eigenspace is weighted by its overlap with $v_b$, and this vector changes with temperature. Periodic concentration therefore leaves a boundary-overlap problem. We solve it by contracting the residual boundary-weighted moments while tracking the physical Gibbs purity (the sum of the squared Gibbs probabilities) of the opposite-field chain. These two estimates improve together when both length and inverse temperature double, without assuming a nonzero boundary overlap at the next temperature. Once the leading spatial line dominates the boundary pairing, its rotation invariance controls the same-field expression at every odd length in a full dyadic interval. The purity defect then decreases at a rate exponential in the inverse temperature, which yields a uniform physical gap.

Sections <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">2</a> and <a href="#sec:periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodic">3</a> establish the transfer representation and the periodic estimates in the form needed here. Section <a href="#sec:boundary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boundary">4</a> proves the boundary recurrence. Section <a href="#sec:initialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:initialization">5</a> initializes and iterates it, and Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a> proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> at all the required odd lengths. Section <a href="#sec:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:topology">7</a> applies the resulting gap to the boundary-selected infinite-volume states. Appendices <a href="#app:thermal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:thermal">8</a> and <a href="#app:trials" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:trials">9</a> give complete finite certificates for the boundary initialization. All terminating decimals used in inequalities denote exact rationals.

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### Spatial transfer with endpoint fields

We first put the periodic and open chains in the same transfer representation. The boundary vectors, including their complex phases, will distinguish the physical endpoint fields. The time-ordered bond expansion has a general formulation in (Aizenman and Nachtergaele 1994, arXiv v1, Section 2.2, Equations (2.10)–(2.12)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[AizenmanNachtergaele1994](#ref-AizenmanNachtergaele1994)]. We prove the required boundary identity directly with the specified kernel and field coefficients.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Hamiltonians and shifted partition functions

<a id="tr:constants"></a>
<a id="tr:Hopp"></a>
From now on set $$\label{tr:constants}
 h=\frac35,\qquad a=\frac{700741}{500000},\qquad
 b_0=\frac{49}{4},\qquad b_j=2^jb_0\quad(j\ge0).$$ It is convenient to label an $n$-site chain by $0,\ldots,n-1$. Write $S_j\cdot S_k=\sum_\alpha S_j^\alpha S_k^\alpha$ and define <a id="tr:Hsame"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 H_n^{\rm opp}&=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}S_j\cdot S_{j+1}
                         -hS_0^z+hS_{n-1}^z,\label{tr:Hopp}\\
 H_n^{\rm s}&=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}S_j\cdot S_{j+1}
                         -hS_0^z-hS_{n-1}^z,\label{tr:Hsame}\\
 H_n^{\rm per}&=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}S_j\cdot S_{j+1}
                         +S_{n-1}\cdot S_0\quad(n\ge4).
\end{aligned}$$ For $n=2L+1$, $H_n^{\rm s}$ is <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a> after relabeling. Our partition functions are ordinary traces over the entire physical Hilbert space: <a id="tr:partitions"></a>$$\label{tr:partitions}
 \begin{split}
 Z_n(b)&=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm per}+anI)},\\
 Y_n(b)&=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm opp}+anI)},\qquad
 Y_n^{\rm s}(b)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{-b(H_n^{\rm s}+anI)}.
 \end{split}$$ The shift is per site, also for open chains. It will cancel from all physical Gibbs weight ratios.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The spatial operator

We use the concrete spatial-transfer operator in (OpenAI 2026, Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]. Its auxiliary space records ordered bond events in imaginary time; each physical site contributes a kernel depending on its two incident histories. Use the unitarily equivalent Cartesian spin matrices $(S^\alpha)_{rs}=-i\varepsilon_{\alpha rs}$ and set $G_\alpha=iS^\alpha$. Then $G_\alpha$ is real, $\lVert G_\alpha\rVert=1$, and $$-S\cdot S=\sum_{\alpha=x,y,z}G_\alpha\otimes G_\alpha.$$ A history is an ordered list $\omega=((t_1,\alpha_1),\ldots,(t_d,\alpha_d))$ with $0<t_1<\cdots<t_d<b$ and $\alpha_r\in\{x,y,z\}$; the empty list is allowed. Give the disjoint union $\Omega_b$ of these labeled simplexes Lebesgue measure on each simplex and mass one at the empty history. Its measure $\mu_b$ has total mass $e^{3b}$.

For two histories, merge their events in chronological order and take the trace of the resulting product of $G$ matrices, with increasing time from left to right. Denote this real kernel by $k_b(\omega,\eta)$. Set it to zero at nonempty time collisions, a null set, and use trace $3$ for the two empty histories. The kernel is symmetric: exchanging the two histories leaves their merged list unchanged. In particular, symmetry does not reverse a product of noncommuting matrices. Moreover $|k_b|\le3$. On $\mathcal K_b=L^2(\Omega_b,\mu_b;\mathbb C)$ define <a id="tr:operator"></a>$$\label{tr:operator}
 (X_bf)(\omega)=e^{-ab}\int_{\Omega_b}k_b(\omega,\eta)
                                      f(\eta)\,d\mu_b(\eta).$$ It is real, self-adjoint, and Hilbert–Schmidt. The cited transfer Proposition identifies its periodic moments: <a id="tr:periodic"></a>$$\label{tr:periodic}
 Z_n(b)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}X_b^n\quad(n\ge4),\qquad
 \sum_i|\mu_i(b)|^k<\infty\quad(k\ge2),$$ where $\mu_i(b)$ lists its nonzero eigenvalues with multiplicity. This exact operator, with the shift in <a href="#tr:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:constants">[tr:constants]</a>, is the one used for the periodic inputs in Section <a href="#sec:periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodic">3</a>.

Rotations will let us change one endpoint field and, later, show that the leading spatial line is fixed. Let $D_2$ consist of the identity and the three diagonal rotations by $\pi$, let $C$ cyclically permute the coordinate axes, and put $\mathcal G=\langle D_2,C\rangle$. This is a group of twelve proper signed permutations. If $ge_\alpha=\epsilon_g(\alpha)
e_{\sigma_g(\alpha)}$, write $\sigma_g\omega$ for relabeling the marks and $s_g(\omega)=\prod_r\epsilon_g(\alpha_r)$. The operators <a id="tr:rotations"></a>$$\label{tr:rotations}
 (U_gf)(\omega)=s_g(\sigma_g^{-1}\omega)f(\sigma_g^{-1}\omega)$$ form a real unitary representation and commute with $X_b$. This follows either directly by rotating every matrix in the kernel, or from the same Proposition. In particular, fix $P=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,-1,-1)$, so $Pe_z=-e_z$.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Boundary vectors

<a id="tr:vector"></a>
Define $v_b\in\mathcal K_b$ by $$\label{tr:vector}
 v_b(\omega)=
 \begin{cases}
 (ih)^d,&\text{all $d$ marks of $\omega$ equal $z$},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ The empty-history value is one. The simplex volumes give $\lVert v_b\rVert^2=\sum_{d\ge0}b^dh^{2d}/d!=e^{bh^2}$. The inner product below is conjugate linear in its first argument.

<a id="stmt-tr-boundary"></a>
<div id="tr:boundary" class="proposition">

<a id="tr:open"></a>
**Proposition 2** (Open-chain transfer). *For $b>0$, $n\ge2$, and $g\in\mathcal G$, the pairing $\langle v_b,X_b^nU_gv_b\rangle$ is the shifted partition function of the open chain with left field $-hS_0^z$ and right field $+hS_{n-1}^{ge_z}$. Consequently $$\label{tr:open}
 Y_n(b)=\langle v_b,X_b^nv_b\rangle,\qquad
 Y_n^{\rm s}(b)=\langle v_b,X_b^nU_Pv_b\rangle.$$ Here $S^u=\sum_\alpha u_\alpha S^\alpha$ for a real vector $u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-boundary"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-boundary</summary>

*Proof.* Expand the negative Hamiltonian exponential into its ordered-time series. Each bond event inserts two $G_\alpha$ matrices, by the Cartesian identity above. At the left endpoint, the conjugated coefficient in <a href="#tr:vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:vector">[tr:vector]</a> gives $(-ih)G_z=+hS^z$ per event; at the right endpoint the coefficient is $(ih)G_z=-hS^z$. Relabeling by $g$ and multiplying its signs changes this last insertion to $-hS^{ge_z}$. These are exactly the two field insertions in the negative Hamiltonian stated in the Proposition.

Regroup the series by the boundary histories $\omega_0,\omega_n$ and the $n-1$ bond histories $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_{n-1}$. Their interleavings partition each full time simplex up to null collisions. At site $r$, $0\le r<n$, the trace is $k_b(\omega_r,\omega_{r+1})$, in the original chronological order. Integrating these $n$ kernel factors against the two boundary coefficients gives $X_b^n$ and the shift factor $e^{-ban}$. No bond joins the endpoints.

This regrouping is legitimate: the absolute sum of traced terms is bounded by $3^n\exp\{b[3(n-1)+2h]\}$ before the scalar shift. All sums and integrals are therefore absolutely convergent. Choosing $g=1$ and $g=P$ gives <a href="#tr:open" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:open">[tr:open]</a>. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:boundary-pairings">

<figcaption>The two spatial boundary pairings. Each box contributes one site kernel; the internal integrations represent the $n − 1$ bonds. Replacing the right boundary vector by $U_{P}v_{b}$ changes the right physical field. This is an identity between transfer representations, not a claim that the two physical Hamiltonians are unitarily equivalent.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-boundary-pairings"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:boundary-pairings`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,
  site/.style={draw,minimum width=9mm,minimum height=8mm},
  every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \foreach \y in {0,-1.8} {
   \node[site] at (0,\y) {$X_b$};
   \node[site] at (1.6,\y) {$X_b$};
   \node at (3.2,\y) {$\cdots$};
   \node[site] at (4.8,\y) {$X_b$};
   \draw (-1.6,\y)--(-.45,\y);
   \draw (.45,\y)--(1.15,\y);
   \draw (2.05,\y)--(2.75,\y);
   \draw (3.65,\y)--(4.35,\y);
   \draw (5.25,\y)--(6.4,\y);
   \node[left] at (-1.6,\y) {$\overline v_b$};
 }
 \node[right] at (6.4,0) {$v_b$};
 \node[right] at (6.4,-1.8) {$U_Pv_b$};
 \node[above] at (-.2,.45) {left: $-hS^z$};
 \node[above] at (5.0,.45) {right: $+hS^z$};
 \node[below] at (-.2,-2.25) {left: $-hS^z$};
 \node[below] at (5.0,-2.25) {right: $-hS^z$};
 \draw[->] (6.7,-.45)--(6.7,-1.35);
 \node[left] at (6.45,-.9) {$P e_z=-e_z$};
 \node at (2.4,-.65) {$n$ site kernels};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 5 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two horizontal 1D transfer-operator diagrams arranged vertically:
- Top diagram: starts on the left with vector $\bar{v}_b$, connected by a horizontal line segment labeled "left: $-hS^z$" to a sequence of boxed nodes $X_b$, $X_b$, $\dots$, $X_b$, and connects on the right by a line segment labeled "right: $+hS^z$" to vector $v_b$. A horizontal brace under the sequence of boxes is labeled "$n\text{ site kernels}$".
- Bottom diagram: starts on the left with vector $\bar{v}_b$, connected by a line segment labeled below with "left: $-hS^z$" to a sequence of boxed nodes $X_b$, $X_b$, $\dots$, $X_b$, and connects on the right by a line segment labeled below with "right: $-hS^z$" to vector $U_P v_b$.
- Between the two diagrams on the right, a vertical downward arrow connects vector $v_b$ in the top row to vector $U_P v_b$ in the bottom row, labeled to its left with "$P e_z = -e_z$".
```


For even lengths, the opposite-field expression is the positive moment $Y_n(b)=\langle v_b,|X_b|^n v_b\rangle$. At odd lengths, the powers of $X_b$ retain the signs of its eigenvalues. We first concentrate the even opposite-field moments. The resulting leading-line estimate will then control the signed same-field pairing at odd lengths; Figure <a href="#fig:boundary-pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:boundary-pairings">1</a> distinguishes the two boundary pairings.

<a id="sec:periodic"></a>
### Periodic concentration estimates

The boundary argument requires quantitative information about the spatial spectrum, including the change of its leading eigenvalue when the inverse temperature doubles. We extract that information from finite periodic-chain estimates in (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]. The extraction is given in detail because the periodic gap theorem alone does not provide these boundary estimates. Throughout, terminating decimals denote exact rational numbers, and eigenvalue lists count multiplicities.

<a id="per:purities"></a>
For even $n\ge4$, put $$\label{per:purities}
 S(n,b)=\frac{Z_{2n}(b)}{Z_n(b)^2},\qquad
 T(n,b)=\frac{Z_n(2b)}{Z_n(b)^2}.$$ The first is the purity of the spatial probabilities $|\mu_i(b)|^n/Z_n(b)$, where $(\mu_i(b))$ is the nonzero spectrum of $X_b$; the second is the purity of the physical Gibbs probabilities. Both lie in $(0,1]$. Doubling $n$ squares the first probability list, whereas doubling $b$ squares the second.

<a id="stmt-per-purity"></a>
<div id="per:purity" class="lemma">

<a id="per:concentration-elementary"></a>
<a id="per:d"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Squaring and concentration). *Let $(w_i)$ be a finite or countable probability list, and suppose its purity is at least $1-u$, where $0\le u<1$. Normalizing the squares $w_i^2$ gives a list with purity defect at most <a id="per:f"></a>$$\label{per:f}
 f(u)=\frac{u^2}{2(1-u)^2}.$$ If $u<1/2$, the largest weight is unique and at least $1-u$. Writing $x$ for the sum of all the other weights, one has $$\label{per:concentration-elementary}
 x\le\frac{1-\sqrt{1-2u}}2.$$ In particular, for $0\le u\le .1$, $$\label{per:d}
 \frac{x}{1-x}\le d(u),\qquad d(u)=\frac{u}{2-3u}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-per-purity"><summary>Proof of stmt-per-purity</summary>

*Proof.* Set $P=\sum_iw_i^2$ and let $P'$ be the purity after squaring. Nonnegativity justifies rearrangement of the countable sums, and $$1-P'=\frac{2\sum_{i<k}w_i^2w_k^2}{P^2}
 \le\frac{2(\sum_{i<k}w_iw_k)^2}{P^2}
 =\frac{(1-P)^2}{2P^2}\le f(u).$$ Here $f$ is increasing on $[0,1)$. A summable nonzero nonnegative list attains its maximum, and $P\le\max_iw_i$. If $u<1/2$, the maximum therefore exceeds $1/2$ and is unique. Thus $x\le u<1/2$, and $$2x(1-x)\le 1-P\le u,$$ which gives <a href="#per:concentration-elementary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:concentration-elementary">[per:concentration-elementary]</a>. Also $x\le u/[2(1-x)]\le u/[2(1-u)]$; applying the increasing function $x\mapsto x/(1-x)$ proves <a href="#per:d" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:d">[per:d]</a>. ◻

</details>

For later reference we also record explicitly the interaction of the two periodic purities. This is (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]; we include its short proof by partition-function cancellation.

<a id="stmt-per-coupled"></a>
<div id="per:coupled" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Coupled update). *Suppose that $n\ge4$ is even, $b>0$, and $S(n,b)\ge s$, $T(2n,b)\ge t$, with $0<s,t\le1$. Set $$p_+=f(1-s^2t).$$ If $p_+<1$, then <a id="per:coupled-formula"></a>$$\label{per:coupled-formula}
 1-S(2n,2b)\le p_+,\qquad
 1-T(4n,2b)\le f\bigl(1-t^2(1-p_+)\bigr).$$ Either upper defect bound may be increased before subsequent use, provided it stays below one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-per-coupled"><summary>Proof of stmt-per-coupled</summary>

*Proof.* Directly from <a href="#per:purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:purities">[per:purities]</a>, $$S(n,2b)=S(n,b)^2\frac{T(2n,b)}{T(n,b)^2}
 \ge s^2t.$$ Apply spatial squaring and Lemma <a href="#per:purity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:purity">3</a>. A second cancellation then gives $$T(4n,b)=T(2n,b)^2\frac{S(2n,2b)}{S(2n,b)^2}
 \ge t^2(1-p_+).$$ Physical squaring proves the second inequality. Monotonicity of $f$ justifies increasing either defect bound. ◻

</details>

We next state the finite inputs gathered in the companion (OpenAI 2026, Corollary 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]. Its shift, spin normalization and unit bilinear coupling are exactly those used here. In particular, we use an intermediate row in the initialization proof, not merely its final conclusion.

<a id="stmt-per-native"></a>
<div id="per:native" class="proposition">

<a id="per:native-first-row"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Finite periodic inputs from (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]). *Set <a id="per:constants"></a>$$\label{per:constants}
 \ell=.999,\quad U=1.00139,\quad V=.872,\quad
 m_J=1.0657205,\quad m_N=.2783155.$$ At $b_0=49/4$, the spatial eigenvalue list decomposes as one list $J$ and three identical lists $N$. Here $J$ is the spectrum on the subspace fixed by every $U_g$, $g\in D_2$, and $N$ is the common spectrum on each of the other three simultaneous eigenspaces of these commuting operators. In $J$ the absolute eigenvalues are at most $U$, and their twelfth powers sum to at most $m_J$; in each $N$ they are at most $V$, and their twelfth powers sum to at most $m_N$. There is an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\ell$. Furthermore, $$\label{per:native-first-row}
 1-S(72,b_1)\le\frac{151249}{2500000}=.0604996.$$ For the periodic chains of $72$ and $120$ sites, the shifted ground energy is strictly negative. Consequently, at each of these lengths and every $b>0$, an individual unnormalized ground Boltzmann weight is greater than one.*

</div>

No boundary Hamiltonian occurs in Proposition <a href="#per:native" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:native">5</a>. The finite periodic thermal and variational certificates supporting it are given in the cited source. We now deduce all the spatial estimates that will be used for boundaries. The residual ratios will contract the boundary-weighted tails. The upper and lower bounds comparing successive leading eigenvalues will control the change of temperature in the boundary concentration and physical-purity estimates, respectively. Finally, positivity and rotation invariance of the leading line will control the same-field pairing at odd lengths.

<a id="stmt-per-concentration"></a>
<div id="per:concentration" class="proposition">

<a id="per:tracked-purities"></a>
<a id="per:spectral-ratio"></a>
<a id="per:lambda-ratios"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Concentration at the doubling scales). *For every $j\ge0$, $X_{b_j}$ has a unique simple eigenvalue of largest modulus. This eigenvalue is positive; denote it by $\lambda_j=\|X_{b_j}\|$. Its eigenspace is fixed pointwise by $\mathcal G$. Let $\rho_j$ be the supremum of the absolute values of all other eigenvalues divided by $\lambda_j$. Then <a id="per:initial-ratios"></a>$$\label{per:initial-ratios}
 \ell<\lambda_0\le U,\qquad
 \rho_0<.874,\qquad \rho_1<.9525,\qquad \rho_2<.971.$$ For $j\ge2$, define <a id="per:scales"></a>$$\label{per:scales}
 n_j=120\,2^{j-2},\qquad M_j=2n_j,\qquad
 u_2=.052,\qquad u_{j+1}=6u_j^2.$$ The following bounds hold for every $j\ge2$: $$\begin{aligned}
 1-S(n_j,b_j)&\le u_j,&
 1-T(M_j,b_j)&\le u_j, \label{per:tracked-purities}\\
 \rho_j^{n_j}&\le d(u_j). &&\label{per:spectral-ratio}
\end{aligned}$$ Finally, put $r_j=\lambda_{j+1}/\lambda_j^2$. Then $$\label{per:lambda-ratios}
 0<r_j\le1\quad(j\ge0),\qquad
 r_j^{M_j}\ge(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1})\quad(j\ge2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-per-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-per-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* Until positivity has been proved, write $\lambda_j=\|X_{b_j}\|$ for the maximal spectral modulus.

*The initial spatial spectrum.* Remove an eigenvalue in $J$ whose modulus exceeds $\ell$. The remaining twelfth-power mass in $J$ is at most $m_J-\ell^{12}<\ell^{12}$, while $V<\ell$ bounds each of the other three sectors. The removed eigenvalue is therefore the unique simple eigenvalue of maximal modulus. The exact rational inequalities $$m_J-\ell^{12}<(.874\ell)^{12},\qquad V<.874\ell$$ prove $\rho_0<.874$.

<a id="per:Z-initial"></a>
We need the total residual moments as well as this individual ratio bound. For every real $k\ge12$, define <a id="per:D"></a>$$\label{per:D}
 D(k)=\frac{(m_J-\ell^{12})^{k/12}
       +3\bigl[V^k+(m_N-V^{12})^{k/12}\bigr]}{\ell^k}.$$ The sum of the $k$th powers of all nondominant absolute eigenvalue ratios at $b_0$ is at most $D(k)$. Indeed, $V^{12}<m_N<2V^{12}$; for a nonnegative list with entries at most $V^{12}$ and total mass at most $m_N$, convexity of $x^{k/12}$ bounds the sum by $(V^{12})^{k/12}+(m_N-V^{12})^{k/12}$. This follows by moving mass to fill one entry before the next; finite sublists followed by a limit give the countable case. Without an entry cap, the corresponding bound is simply the total mass to the power $k/12$. Applying these facts to the four sectors proves the claim. In particular, for even $k\ge12$, $$\label{per:Z-initial}
 Z_k(b_0)\le U^k(1+D(k)).$$

*Reseeding at $(120,b_2)$.* Our next goal is to make both $1-S(120,b_2)$ and $1-T(240,b_2)$ at most $.052$, so that one quadratic recurrence will control them. A coupled update will give these bounds from spatial and physical seeds at $(60,b_1)$ and $(120,b_1)$, respectively. To obtain the spatial seed, we combine a leading-eigenvalue lower bound from length $72$ with a residual-moment upper bound at length $32$, then increase the moment order to $60$. The same residual estimate also supplies the ratio $\rho_1$ needed in the first boundary updates.

<a id="per:lambda1"></a>
<a id="per:tail32"></a>
<a id="per:seed-s"></a>
By <a href="#per:native-first-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:native-first-row">[per:native-first-row]</a> and Lemma <a href="#per:purity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:purity">3</a>, the spatial list at $(72,b_1)$ has a unique largest weight. Thus $X_{b_1}$ also has a unique simple eigenvalue of maximal modulus. Moreover, $$.0604996<2(.032)(.968)$$ implies that this weight exceeds $.968$. The periodic trial at length $72$ gives $Z_{72}(b_1)>1$, so $$\label{per:lambda1}
 \lambda_1^{72}>.968.$$ Physical squaring at length $32$ gives $Z_{32}(b_1)\le Z_{32}(b_0)^2$. Subtracting the dominant spatial contribution and using <a href="#per:Z-initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:Z-initial">[per:Z-initial]</a> and <a href="#per:lambda1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:lambda1">[per:lambda1]</a> therefore yields $$\label{per:tail32}
 \sum_{i\ne0}\left|\frac{\mu_i(b_1)}{\lambda_1}\right|^{32}
 \le U^{64}(1+D(32))^2(.968)^{-32/72}-1
 <.9525^{32}.$$ This proves $\rho_1<.9525$. For any nonnegative list $(a_i)$ and $q\ge p>0$, one has $\sum_i a_i^q\le(\sum_i a_i^p)^{q/p}$ whenever the right side is finite. Applying this to <a href="#per:tail32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:tail32">[per:tail32]</a> gives a residual $60$th moment below $.9525^{60}$. Retaining only the dominant term in the numerator of $S(60,b_1)$ proves $$\label{per:seed-s}
 S(60,b_1)\ge s:=(1+.9525^{60})^{-2}.$$

<a id="per:seed-t"></a>
For the physical spectrum at length $120$, choose one ground Boltzmann weight at $b_0$ and divide every other weight by it. The chosen weight is greater than one by Proposition <a href="#per:native" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:native">5</a>. Hence the sum of the other ratios is bounded above by $$R=U^{120}(1+D(120))-1.$$ When $b$ doubles, these ratios square, so their sum is at most $R^2$. Keeping the chosen ground weight in the purity numerator now gives $$\label{per:seed-t}
 T(120,b_1)\ge t:=(1+R^2)^{-2}.$$ This reasoning neither assumes ground-state simplicity nor discards multiplicities.

Here are rational checks sufficient for every numerical comparison just used and for the ensuing seed: <a id="per:scalar-checks"></a>$$\label{per:scalar-checks}
\begin{array}{rcl}
 D(32)&<&.0442,\\
 D(120)&<&.00000025,\\
 (.9856)^9&<&(.968)^4,\\
 U^{64}(1+.0442)^2/.9856-1&<&.2092<.9525^{32},\\
 s&>&.9002,\qquad t>.9372,\\
 f(1-.9002^2\,.9372)&<&.0502,\\
 f(1-.9372^2(1-.0502))&<&.0198.
\end{array}$$ All comparisons are between explicit rational numbers after bounding the cube roots in $D(32)$ by $$(m_J-\ell^{12})^{1/3}<.426635,\qquad
 (m_N-V^{12})^{1/3}<.439736.$$ Cubing verifies these two bounds; raising the bounds to the eighth power gives the displayed bound on $D(32)$. For $D(120)$ no roots are needed. The bound on $t$ follows already with $D(120)$ replaced by $.00000025$. Lemma <a href="#per:coupled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:coupled">4</a>, applied to <a href="#per:seed-s" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:seed-s">[per:seed-s]</a>–<a href="#per:seed-t" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:seed-t">[per:seed-t]</a> at $(n,b)=(60,b_1)$, now gives $$1-S(120,b_2)<.052,\qquad 1-T(240,b_2)<.052.$$

*Induction.* For $0\le u\le .052$, <a id="per:G"></a>$$\label{per:G}
 f\bigl(1-(1-u)^3\bigr)=G(u)u^2,\qquad
 G(u)=\frac12\left(\sum_{r=1}^3(1-u)^{-r}\right)^2.$$ The function $G$ is increasing, and exact substitution gives $G(.052)<5.583<6$. Also $6u^2\le u$ on this interval. Thus, if the two defects in <a href="#per:tracked-purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:tracked-purities">[per:tracked-purities]</a> are at most $u_j$, the first coupled output is at most $6u_j^2=u_{j+1}\le u_j$. Using this upper bound in the second output bounds that output by the same number. This proves <a href="#per:tracked-purities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:tracked-purities">[per:tracked-purities]</a> for every $j\ge2$. Each spatial purity exceeds $1/2$, so the maximal spatial modulus remains unique and simple. Applied to its probability list, Lemma <a href="#per:purity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:purity">3</a> also gives $$\sum_{i\ne0}\left|\frac{\mu_i(b_j)}{\lambda_j}\right|^{n_j}
 \le d(u_j),$$ which proves <a href="#per:spectral-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:spectral-ratio">[per:spectral-ratio]</a>. The rational comparison $d(.052)<.971^{120}$ gives $\rho_2<.971$.

*The sign and the rotation symmetry.* At every level we have proved uniqueness of the eigenvalue of maximal modulus. If it were $-\lambda_j$, then $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(X_{b_j}^n)/\lambda_j^n$ would tend to $-1$ through odd integers $n$. Indeed, the residual ratios are bounded by some number below one, and their fourth powers have finite sum, so their $n$th-power sum vanishes as $n\to\infty$. This contradicts the strictly positive physical partition function $Z_n(b_j)$ at odd $n\ge5$. The leading eigenvalue is therefore $+\lambda_j$.

Its eigenspace has a real unit vector, since $X_{b_j}$ is real. The real unitaries $U_g$ commute with $X_{b_j}$, so they act on that line by signs. The order-three cyclic rotation $C$ must act by $+1$. It conjugates the three nonidentity elements of $D_2$ cyclically, so they have the same sign on the line. Since the product of any two is the third, that common sign equals its square and hence is $+1$. Thus every element of $\mathcal G=\langle D_2,C\rangle$ fixes the leading eigenspace pointwise.

*Comparing the leading eigenvalues.* For every even $N\ge4$, physical squaring gives $Z_N(b_{j+1})\le Z_N(b_j)^2$. The residual-tail estimate used above gives $Z_N(b_j)/\lambda_j^N=1+o(1)$, so $Z_N(b_j)^{1/N}\to\lambda_j$. Taking $N$th roots and letting $N$ tend to infinity through even integers therefore yields $\lambda_{j+1}\le\lambda_j^2$. For the opposite direction at the reference scale, recall that $M_j=n_{j+1}$. The leading spatial probability at $(M_j,b_{j+1})$ is at least $1-u_{j+1}$, so $$\begin{split}
 \lambda_{j+1}^{M_j}
 &\ge(1-u_{j+1})Z_{M_j}(b_{j+1})\\
 &= (1-u_{j+1})T(M_j,b_j)Z_{M_j}(b_j)^2\\
 &\ge(1-u_{j+1})(1-u_j)\lambda_j^{2M_j}.
 \end{split}$$ This proves <a href="#per:lambda-ratios" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="per:lambda-ratios">[per:lambda-ratios]</a> and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:boundary"></a>
### Concentration of boundary-weighted moments

We now pass from unweighted spatial traces to the boundary pairing for the opposite-field chain. We will control two quantities together: the fraction of an even boundary moment outside the leading spatial line, and the defect of the physical Gibbs purity. The boundary coefficients depend on temperature. We compare the weighted moments across temperatures through physical partition-function identities, while contracting the residual spatial powers at a fixed temperature.

<a id="be:definitions"></a>
Use the simple positive leading eigenvalue $\lambda_j$ and residual ratio $\rho_j$ from Section <a href="#sec:periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodic">3</a>. Let $P_j$ be the orthogonal projection onto its line, and put $$\label{be:definitions}
 \begin{split}
 c_j&=\lVert P_jv_{b_j}\rVert^2,\\
 B_k(j)&=\langle v_{b_j},(|X_{b_j}|/\lambda_j)^k v_{b_j}\rangle,
 \qquad e_k(j)=\frac{B_k(j)-c_j}{B_k(j)},\\
 A(m,j)&=\frac{Y_m(b_{j+1})}{Y_m(b_j)^2}.
 \end{split}$$ We use $k,m$ even and at least two. Then $B_k(j)=Y_k(b_j)/\lambda_j^k>0$, so $e_k(j)$ is defined even if $c_j=0$. Also $0\le e_k(j)\le1$ and $0<A(m,j)\le1$. Thus $e_k(j)$ is the residual fraction of a spatial boundary moment, whereas $A(m,j)$ is the physical Gibbs purity of the opposite-field chain at inverse temperature $b_j$.

<a id="be:residual"></a>
The normalized absolute spatial eigenvalues are at most one. Thus $B_m(j)\le B_k(j)$ when $m\ge k$. More precisely, if $D_k(j)=B_k(j)-c_j$, the spectral theorem gives $$\label{be:residual}
 0\le D_m(j)\le\rho_j^{m-k}D_k(j)\qquad(m\ge k).$$ The powers act on the residual spectrum; no upper bound for the total boundary norm is needed in this comparison.

<a id="stmt-be-recurrence"></a>
<div id="be:recurrence" class="lemma">

<a id="be:step"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Transfer between temperatures). *Let $j\ge0$ and let $m>k\ge2$ be even. Suppose $e_k(j)<1$ and $0<q<1$ with $q\ge\rho_{j+1}^{m-k}$. Define, for $0\le e<1$ and $0<t\le1$, <a id="be:F"></a>$$\label{be:F}
 F(q,e,t)=\frac{q}{1-q}\left(\frac{1}{(1-e)^2t}-1\right).$$ Then $$\label{be:step}
 e_m(j+1)\le F(q,e_k(j),A(m,j)).$$ In particular, an upper bound strictly below one on the right proves $c_{j+1}>0$ without presupposing it.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-be-recurrence"><summary>Proof of stmt-be-recurrence</summary>

*Proof.* At the new temperature, <a href="#be:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:residual">[be:residual]</a> yields $B_m(j+1)-c_{j+1}\le q[B_k(j+1)-c_{j+1}]$. Subtract and rearrange to obtain $$e_m(j+1)\le\frac{q}{1-q}
                   \left(\frac{B_k(j+1)}{B_m(j+1)}-1\right).$$ Write $r_j=\lambda_{j+1}/\lambda_j^2$. From the definitions, <a id="be:ratio"></a>$$\label{be:ratio}
 \frac{B_k(j+1)}{B_m(j+1)}
 =\frac{A(k,j)}{A(m,j)}
   \left(\frac{B_k(j)}{B_m(j)}\right)^2 r_j^{m-k}.$$ Section <a href="#sec:periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodic">3</a> gives $r_j\le1$, and physical purity gives $A(k,j)\le1$. The hypothesis $e_k(j)<1$ implies $c_j>0$, and $B_m(j)\ge c_j$ gives $B_k(j)/B_m(j)\le(1-e_k(j))^{-1}$. Substitution proves <a href="#be:step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:step">[be:step]</a>. Only the old overlap $c_j$ has been divided by. The new overlap follows from $e_m(j+1)<1$ and $B_m(j+1)>0$. ◻

</details>

To couple this residual estimate to physical purity, we use a shorter reference length for the boundary defect. Recall $M_j=240\,2^{j-2}$ and $u_j$ from Proposition <a href="#per:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:concentration">6</a>. For $j\ge2$ let <a id="be:k"></a>$$\label{be:k}
 k_2=142,\qquad k_{j+1}=M_j.$$ We track upper bounds $E_j$ on $e_{k_j}(j)$ and $\eta_j$ on $1-A(M_j,j)$. The difference $M_j-k_j$ supplies residual contraction at the next temperature. Its output at length $k_{j+1}=M_j$ then controls the ratio $B_{2M_j}(j+1)/B_{M_j}(j+1)$ needed to improve physical purity. The notation $\eta_j$ denotes only this physical purity defect.

<a id="stmt-be-coupled"></a>
<div id="be:coupled" class="proposition">

<a id="be:Eupdate"></a>
<a id="be:etaupdate"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Coupled boundary update). *Suppose $j\ge2$, $0\le E_j,\eta_j<1$, and $$e_{k_j}(j)\le E_j,\qquad A(M_j,j)\ge1-\eta_j.$$ Let $E_{j+1},\eta_{j+1}\in[0,1)$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 E_{j+1}&\ge
 F\left(d(u_{j+1})^{(M_j-k_j)/M_j},E_j,1-\eta_j\right),
                                                   \label{be:Eupdate}\\
 \eta_{j+1}&\ge f\left(1-(1-\eta_j)(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1})
                                      (1-E_{j+1})\right).
                                                   \label{be:etaupdate}
\end{aligned}$$ Then $e_{k_{j+1}}(j+1)\le E_{j+1}$ and $A(M_{j+1},j+1)\ge1-\eta_{j+1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-be-coupled"><summary>Proof of stmt-be-coupled</summary>

*Proof.* Here $M_j>k_j$ and $n_{j+1}=M_j$. The periodic residual bound gives $$\rho_{j+1}^{M_j-k_j}\le d(u_{j+1})^{(M_j-k_j)/M_j}<1.$$ The function $F$ increases in $q,e$ and decreases in $t$ on the stated domains. Lemma <a href="#be:recurrence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="be:recurrence">7</a> therefore proves <a href="#be:Eupdate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:Eupdate">[be:Eupdate]</a>’s conclusion, including $c_{j+1}>0$.
