Put $M=M_j$. Exact cancellation in <a href="#be:definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:definitions">[be:definitions]</a> gives <a id="be:physical-cancellation"></a>$$\label{be:physical-cancellation}
 \frac{A(2M,j)}{A(M,j)}
 =r_j^M\frac{B_{2M}(j+1)}{B_M(j+1)}
             \left(\frac{B_M(j)}{B_{2M}(j)}\right)^2.$$ The squared factor is at least one, and the first boundary ratio is at least $c_{j+1}/B_M(j+1)\ge1-E_{j+1}$. Thus Proposition <a href="#per:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:concentration">6</a> gives $$A(2M,j)\ge(1-\eta_j)(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1})(1-E_{j+1}).$$ At the fixed physical length $2M$, passing from $b_j$ to $b_{j+1}$ normalizes the squares of the Gibbs probabilities. Lemma <a href="#per:purity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:purity">3</a> bounds the resulting defect by $f$ of the preceding defect. Since $M_{j+1}=2M$, this is <a href="#be:etaupdate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:etaupdate">[be:etaupdate]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:initialization"></a>
### Finite initialization and quadratic improvement

We now initialize the boundary recurrence and show that it continues indefinitely. The sixth boundary moment starts a sequence of residual estimates at $(34,b_0)$, $(78,b_1)$, and $(142,b_2)$. A physical-purity estimate at $(240,b_2)$ then starts the coupled update. The inputs are four variational energies and thermal moments on at most six sites.

<a id="stmt-in-finite"></a>
<div id="in:finite" class="proposition">

<a id="in:trials"></a>
<a id="in:thermal"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Finite boundary data). *Let $\alpha=.4042$ and $P_*(x)=x^3-.14x^2-.18x$. For the opposite-field Hamiltonian <a href="#tr:Hopp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Hopp">[tr:Hopp]</a>, $$\label{in:trials}
 E_0(H_m^{\rm opp})+am<\alpha\qquad(m=34,78,142,240).$$ At inverse temperature $b_0$, $$\label{in:thermal}
 Y_6(b_0)<.027,\qquad c_0P_*(\lambda_0)^2<.003745.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-in-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-in-finite</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#apptr:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:bound">17</a> proves <a href="#in:trials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:trials">[in:trials]</a> with explicit integer trial vectors. Proposition <a href="#appth:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="appth:bounds">15</a> proves <a href="#in:thermal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:thermal">[in:thermal]</a> by finite thermal recurrences with certified rounding errors. The appendices give all matrix and boundary data and the precise finite inequalities. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Starting residual and physical-purity bounds

<a id="in:epsR"></a>
The thermal estimates bound the residual and leading contributions to $Y_m(b_0)$, while the variational estimate supplies a lower bound for one ground weight. Keep $\ell,U$ from the periodic seed and define, for each of the four lengths in <a href="#in:trials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:trials">[in:trials]</a>, $$\label{in:epsR}
 \epsilon(m)=.027e^{b_0\alpha}U^m\frac{.874^{m-6}}{\ell^6},
 \qquad R(m)=e^{.132}U^m+\epsilon(m)-1.$$

<a id="stmt-in-seedbounds"></a>
<div id="in:seedbounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *At these four lengths, <a id="in:seedineq"></a>$$\label{in:seedineq}
 e_m(0)\le\epsilon(m),\qquad
 A(m,j)\ge\bigl(1+R(m)^{2^j}\bigr)^{-2}\quad(j\ge0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-in-seedbounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-in-seedbounds</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#be:residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:residual">[be:residual]</a>, $\rho_0\le.874$, and $\ell<\lambda_0\le U$, $$\lambda_0^mD_m(0)
 \le U^m .874^{m-6}\frac{Y_6(b_0)}{\ell^6}
 <e^{-b_0\alpha}\epsilon(m).$$ The trial bound supplies a physical ground weight greater than $e^{-b_0\alpha}$ and hence $Y_m(b_0)>e^{-b_0\alpha}$. Dividing gives the first inequality.

To bound the full physical partition function from above, factor $P_*(x)=x(x-1/2)(x+9/25)$. On $[\ell,U]$ it is positive and $xP_*'(x)<4P_*(x)$. For example the latter is the positivity of $4P_*(x)-xP_*'(x)=x(x^2-.28x-.54)$ on this interval. It follows that $x^m/P_*(x)^2$ is increasing for $m>8$. Consequently <a href="#in:thermal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:thermal">[in:thermal]</a> gives $$e^{b_0\alpha}\lambda_0^mc_0
 \le U^m\frac{.003745e^{b_0\alpha}}{P_*(U)^2}
 <e^{.132}U^m.$$ The last scalar comparison has left coefficient below $1.133226$, whereas $e^{.132}>1.141108$. Combining the leading and residual estimates yields $e^{b_0\alpha}Y_m(b_0)\le1+R(m)$.

Select one physical ground eigenvector; uniqueness is not needed. Its unnormalized weight $g$ is greater than $e^{-b_0\alpha}$, so the sum of the remaining weights divided by $g$ is at most $R(m)$. In particular $R(m)\ge0$. At $b_j=2^jb_0$, the corresponding rest-to-ground ratio is at most $R(m)^{2^j}$, since a sum of nonnegative $2^j$th powers is at most the $2^j$th power of the sum. The purity is at least the square of the normalized weight of this chosen ground eigenvector. This proves the second inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="in:78"></a>
<a id="in:142"></a>
<a id="in:initial"></a>
In particular $\epsilon(34)<.0928<1$, proving $c_0>0$. The estimates $\rho_1\le.953$ and $\rho_2\le.971$ now allow two applications of Lemma <a href="#be:recurrence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="be:recurrence">7</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 e_{78}(1)&\le F\bigl(.953^{44},.093,(1+R(78))^{-2}\bigr)<.133,
                                                     \label{in:78}\\
 e_{142}(2)&\le F\bigl(.971^{64},.133,(1+R(142)^2)^{-2}\bigr)<.14.
                                                     \label{in:142}
\end{aligned}$$ The bound at $m=240$, $j=2$ gives $1-A(240,2)<.21$. Thus the induction of Proposition <a href="#be:coupled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="be:coupled">8</a> starts with $$\label{in:initial}
 E_2=.14,\qquad\eta_2=.21.$$ Both new leading overlaps have also been proved positive by the strict defect bounds; no overlap was assumed at the next temperature.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Explicit finite comparisons

<a id="in:puritynumbers"></a>
We record numerical margins to make the initialization reproducible. The three lower bounds from Lemma <a href="#in:seedbounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="in:seedbounds">10</a> used above are $$\label{in:puritynumbers}
 \begin{split}
 (1+R(78))^{-2}&>.6180,\\
 (1+R(142)^2)^{-2}&>.7533,\\
 (1+R(240)^4)^{-2}&>.7923.
 \end{split}$$ Using the full expressions in <a href="#in:epsR" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:epsR">[in:epsR]</a> gives upper outputs below $.132129$, $.137301$, and $.207518$ in <a href="#in:78" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:78">[in:78]</a>, <a href="#in:142" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:142">[in:142]</a>, and $1-A(240,2)$ respectively. The lower bounds in <a href="#in:puritynumbers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:puritynumbers">[in:puritynumbers]</a> are rounded for display.

<a id="in:table"></a>
Starting from <a href="#in:initial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:initial">[in:initial]</a>, the following rounded bounds satisfy Proposition <a href="#be:coupled" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="be:coupled">8</a>: $$\label{in:table}
\begin{array}{c|cc|c}
 j&E_j&\eta_j&\text{upper bound on the $q$ used for step $j\to j+1$}\\\hline
 2&.14&.21&.142\\
 3&.12&.155&.0282\\
 4&.016&.026&.0028\\
 5&.001&.001&\text{---}
\end{array}$$ The exponents $(M_j-k_j)/M_j$ are $98/240$, $1/2$, $1/2$. The successive upper outputs for $E_{j+1}$ are below $.117754,.015328,.000170$; those for $\eta_{j+1}$ are below $.147078,.025188,.000432$. In computing the latter outputs one uses the displayed rounded $E_{j+1}$, so each line closes with the next.

For clarity, all the scalar comparisons here and in the periodic initialization have an elementary rational verification. For $0\le t\le10$, put $T_{80}(t)=\sum_{r=0}^{80}t^r/r!$. Then <a id="in:exp-enclosure"></a>$$\label{in:exp-enclosure}
 T_{80}(t)\le e^t\le T_{80}(t)+2t^{81}/81!.$$ Indeed the ratio of consecutive omitted terms is at most $10/82<1/2$. For negative exponents use reciprocal intervals. Rational positive root comparisons can be checked by raising both sides to their integer powers; for example the first $q$ bound in <a href="#in:table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:table">[in:table]</a> is $d(u_3)^{49}<.142^{120}$. Every denominator is positive. These rules, exact rational input values, and the displayed formulas suffice to verify the finite comparisons without floating-point assumptions. The supplementary scalar certificate records all of them.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Indefinite continuation

We now enlarge the three defect bounds at $j=5$ to $.01$ so that a single quadratic recurrence controls all later levels.

<a id="stmt-in-quadratic"></a>
<div id="in:quadratic" class="proposition">

<a id="in:common"></a>
<a id="in:xexplicit"></a>
**Proposition 11**. *Define $x_5=.01$ and $x_{j+1}=10x_j^2$ for $j\ge5$. Then, for every $j\ge5$, $$\label{in:common}
 u_j\le x_j,\qquad e_{k_j}(j)\le x_j,\qquad
 1-A(M_j,j)\le x_j.$$ In particular $$\label{in:xexplicit}
 x_j=10^{-1-2^{j-5}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-in-quadratic"><summary>Proof of stmt-in-quadratic</summary>

*Proof.* The table proves the two boundary inequalities at $j=5$, and $u_5<.000015<.01$. Suppose <a href="#in:common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:common">[in:common]</a> holds, and write $x=x_j\le.01$. Here $k_j=M_j/2$ and $u_{j+1}=6u_j^2\le6x^2$. The first argument of $F$ in <a href="#be:Eupdate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:Eupdate">[be:Eupdate]</a> satisfies $$\sqrt{d(u_{j+1})}\le
 \sqrt{\frac{6x^2}{2-18x^2}}\le2x.$$ The first output is therefore bounded by $F(2x,x,1-x)$. For $x>0$, division by $x^2$ gives $$\frac{F(2x,x,1-x)}{x^2}
 =\frac{2}{1-2x}\bigl((1-x)^{-1}+(1-x)^{-2}+(1-x)^{-3}\bigr).$$ This expression is increasing on $[0,.01]$ after its continuous extension at zero. Its endpoint value is $42430000/6792093<10$. Thus we may take $E_{j+1}=10x^2$.

For factors in $[0,1]$, the defect of their product is at most the sum of their defects. Thus the four-factor product in <a href="#be:etaupdate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="be:etaupdate">[be:etaupdate]</a> has defect at most $x+x+6x^2+10x^2=2x+16x^2$. Moreover $$\frac{f(2x+16x^2)}{x^2}
 =\frac{(2+16x)^2}{2(1-2x-16x^2)^2}$$ is increasing on $[0,.01]$ and has endpoint value $3645000/1495729<10$. Thus we may also take $\eta_{j+1}=10x^2$. Finally $u_{j+1}\le6x^2\le10x^2$. This proves <a href="#in:common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:common">[in:common]</a> at the next level; the sequence remains in $[0,.01]$. Solving its recurrence gives <a href="#in:xexplicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:xexplicit">[in:xexplicit]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Same fields, odd lengths, and the physical gap

The estimates obtained so far concern even moments of the opposite-field chain. We now use the rotation-invariant leading line to control the same-field partition function at every odd length in a dyadic interval.

<a id="stmt-co-signed"></a>
<div id="co:signed" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Signed boundary comparison). *For $j\ge0$, an even $M\ge2$, and any integer $n\ge M$, define $Q_j(n)=Y_n^{\rm s}(b_j)/\lambda_j^n$. Then <a id="co:error"></a>$$\label{co:error}
 |Q_j(n)-c_j|\le B_M(j)-c_j.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-signed"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-signed</summary>

*Proof.* The leading spatial line is fixed by $U_P$, and its eigenvalue is $+\lambda_j$. Thus its contribution to $Q_j(n)$ is exactly $c_j$. For every other eigenvalue $\xi$ of $X_{b_j}$, let $P_\xi$ be its spectral projection. The unitary $U_P$ commutes with that projection, so Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$|\langle P_\xi v_{b_j},U_P P_\xi v_{b_j}\rangle|
 \le\lVert P_\xi v_{b_j}\rVert^2.$$ Consequently the absolute residual is at most $$\sum_{\xi\ne\lambda_j}
       (|\xi|/\lambda_j)^n\lVert P_\xi v_{b_j}\rVert^2
 \le B_M(j)-c_j.$$ The series converges absolutely, being bounded by $\lVert v_{b_j}\rVert^2$. The kernel of $X_{b_j}$ contributes zero because all exponents are positive. Negative spatial eigenvalues and odd powers are therefore included in the estimate. ◻

</details>

One concentrated even moment therefore controls the normalized same-field pairing $Q_j(n)$ at every longer length, including odd lengths. To compare two temperatures, we use the common even reference length $M_j=k_{j+1}$. The lower bound for $r_j^{M_j}$ will cover the interval $M_j\le n\le2M_j$.

<a id="stmt-co-purity"></a>
<div id="co:purity" class="proposition">

<a id="co:purity-bound"></a>
**Proposition 13**. *For $j\ge5$ and every integer $n$ with $M_j\le n\le2M_j$, $$\label{co:purity-bound}
 \frac{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_{j+1})}{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_j)^2}\ge1-4x_j.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-purity"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-purity</summary>

*Proof.* Put $M=M_j$. At temperature $b_j$, Lemma <a href="#co:signed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:signed">12</a> gives $Q_j(n)\le B_M(j)$. This upper bound can be squared because the physical partition function, and hence $Q_j(n)$, is positive. At temperature $b_{j+1}$, use the same even length $M=k_{j+1}$: $$Q_{j+1}(n)\ge2c_{j+1}-B_M(j+1)
 \ge(1-2x_{j+1})B_M(j+1)>0.$$ The last step uses Proposition <a href="#in:quadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="in:quadratic">11</a>. It follows that <a id="co:product"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_{j+1})}{Y_n^{\rm s}(b_j)^2}
 &\ge(1-2x_{j+1})A(M,j)r_j^{n-M} \notag\\
 &\ge(1-2x_{j+1})(1-x_j)(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1}).
                                                    \label{co:product}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $0<r_j\le1$ and $0\le n-M\le M$ give $r_j^{n-M}\ge r_j^M\ge(1-u_j)(1-u_{j+1})$. Using $x_{j+1}=10x_j^2$, $u_j\le x_j$, and $u_{j+1}\le6x_j^2$, the defect of the last product is at most $$20x_j^2+x_j+x_j+6x_j^2
 =2x_j+26x_j^2\le4x_j,$$ as $x_j\le.01$. This proves <a href="#co:purity-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:purity-bound">[co:purity-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-purity-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-purity</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix an odd $n$ in $[M_j,2M_j]$ with $j\ge5$. Let $p_0\ge p_1\ge\cdots$ be the Gibbs probabilities of $H_n^{\rm s}$ at inverse temperature $b_j$, listing energies with multiplicity. The left side of <a href="#co:purity-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:purity-bound">[co:purity-bound]</a> is $\sum_i p_i^2$. Since $\sum_i p_i^2\le p_0\sum_i p_i=p_0$, we have $p_0\ge1-4x_j>1/2$. In particular the physical ground state is simple, and <a id="co:ratio"></a>$$\label{co:ratio}
 e^{-b_j(E_1-E_0)}=\frac{p_1}{p_0}
 \le\frac{1-p_0}{p_0}
 \le\frac{4x_j}{1-4x_j}
 <10^{-2^{j-5}}.$$ For the strict last inequality, use <a href="#in:xexplicit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="in:xexplicit">[in:xexplicit]</a> and $4/[10(1-.04)]<1$. Because $b_j=392\,2^{j-5}$, taking logarithms yields $E_1-E_0>\log(10)/392$.

Finally $M_5=1920$ and $M_{j+1}=2M_j$. The intervals $[M_j,2M_j]$, $j\ge5$, cover every integer $n\ge1920$. Every odd length $n=2L+1$ with $L\ge960$ is therefore covered. Relabeling the sites gives precisely <a href="#eq:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target">[eq:target]</a>, with the single field $h=3/5$ fixed in <a href="#tr:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:constants">[tr:constants]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:topology"></a>
### The boundary-selected infinite-volume state

The gap just proved supplies the hypothesis of Tasaki’s topological-index theorem. We formulate the consequence for subsequential local limits, so that no convergence or uniqueness of the infinite-volume state is presupposed.

<a id="top:limit"></a>
A local observable is a matrix acting on finitely many sites of $\mathbb Z$, extended by the identity on the other sites. A state on these observables is a normalized positive linear functional. Write $\phi_L$ for a normalized ground vector of $H_L(3/5)$ and set $\omega_L(A)=\langle\phi_L,A\phi_L\rangle$ whenever the support of $A$ lies in $[-L,L]$. For $L\ge960$ the vector is unique up to phase by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, so this functional is well defined. Compactness of the density matrices on each finite interval, followed by a diagonal subsequence over the intervals, gives integers $L_k\to\infty$ for which $$\label{top:limit}
 \omega(A)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\omega_{L_k}(A)$$ exists for every local observable. The limits are consistent on nested intervals and therefore define a state. We call any such state *boundary-selected*.

<a id="top:twist"></a>
The bulk Hamiltonian is the formal sum $H_{\rm bulk}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}S_j\cdot S_{j+1}$. For a local $A$, its commutator $[H_{\rm bulk},A]$ is the finite sum of the commutators with bonds meeting the support of $A$. To specify the index, for $\varepsilon>0$ define the local twist $$\label{top:twist}
 U_\varepsilon=
 \exp\left[-i\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z\cap[-\pi/\varepsilon,\pi/\varepsilon]}
                 (\pi+\varepsilon j)S_j^z\right].$$ The rotations by $0$ and $2\pi$ outside this interval act trivially for spin one. For the symmetric gapped states considered below, Tasaki’s theorem guarantees the existence of the index $\operatorname{Ind}(\omega)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
\omega(U_\varepsilon)\in\{-1,1\}$; we use precisely the twist convention in (Tasaki 2025, Equations (10)–(13)) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)].

<a id="stmt-cor-topology"></a>
<div id="cor:topology" class="corollary">

<a id="top:local-gap"></a>
**Corollary 14**. *Every boundary-selected state <a href="#top:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:limit">[top:limit]</a> is invariant under uniform rotations about the $z$ axis and under reflection $j\mapsto-j$. With $\gamma_*=(\log10)/392$, it satisfies $$\label{top:local-gap}
 \omega\bigl(A^*[H_{\rm bulk},A]\bigr)
 \ge\gamma_*\bigl(\omega(A^*A)-|\omega(A)|^2\bigr)
 \qquad\text{for every local }A.$$ In particular it is a locally-unique gapped ground state in the sense of (Tasaki 2025, Definition 11) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)], and its Tasaki index is $-1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-topology"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-topology</summary>

*Proof.* Both finite-volume symmetries commute with $H_L(3/5)$. Simplicity of the ground eigenspace implies invariance of $\omega_L$ under them; passing to <a href="#top:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:limit">[top:limit]</a> proves the asserted invariances.

For $L\ge960$, the finite spectral theorem and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> give $$\langle A\phi_L,(H_L(3/5)-E_0(L,3/5))A\phi_L\rangle
 \ge\gamma_*\bigl(\omega_L(A^*A)-|\omega_L(A)|^2\bigr).$$ The expression on the left is $\omega_L(A^*[H_L(3/5),A])$. Once the support of $A$ and its adjacent sites lie strictly inside $[-L,L]$, this commutator equals $[H_{\rm bulk},A]$: the endpoint fields and all omitted bonds commute with $A$. Taking the limit proves <a href="#top:local-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:local-gap">[top:local-gap]</a>. Its right side is nonnegative by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for a state; for $\omega(A)=0$ it is the local gap inequality. The strict finite-volume lower bound has consequently yielded the non-strict lower bound $\gamma_*$ in the limit.

Finally the Hamiltonians, spin normalization and endpoint-field signs are exactly those of (Tasaki 2025, Equation (2)) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> verifies that paper’s Assumption 2 with the single field $h=3/5$, $L_0=960$ and $\gamma=\gamma_*$. Its Theorem 3, with the subsequential interpretation specified in its End Matter, Equation (27), therefore applies to every state <a href="#top:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:limit">[top:limit]</a>. To make the sign and limit order explicit, Tasaki’s finite-chain twist interpolation begins at the uniform $\pi$ rotation. Its expectation is $-1$, because the finite ground state has total $S^z=1$ by that paper’s Lemma 1. Reflection makes the twist expectation real, and the uniform gap prevents it from crossing zero while the twist parameter is sufficiently small. More precisely, (Tasaki 2025, Lemmas 6–8 and Equations (17)–(18)) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)] gives, for every sufficiently small fixed $\varepsilon>0$ and all sufficiently large $L$, $$-1\le\omega_L(U_\varepsilon)
 \le-\sqrt{1-\frac{4(\pi+\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{\gamma_*}}.$$ Here $L\ge\pi/\varepsilon$ ensures that the finite-chain twist equals the local operator <a href="#top:twist" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:twist">[top:twist]</a>. Passing first to <a href="#top:limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="top:limit">[top:limit]</a> and then letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ gives $\operatorname{Ind}(\omega)=-1$. ◻

</details>

The corollary evaluates the twist index for the selected states. It does not require the full sequence $\omega_L$ to converge or identify these states with every ground state of the infinite chain. In particular, the additional global uniqueness hypothesis of the half-chain conclusion in (Tasaki 2025, Corollary 4) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Tasaki2025](#ref-Tasaki2025)] is not part of our result.

<a id="app:thermal"></a>
### Certified short-chain thermal moments

This appendix proves the two short-chain estimates used to initialize the boundary argument. Throughout, $h=3/5$, $a=700741/500000$, and $b_0=49/4$. All terminating decimals below denote exact rational numbers.

<a id="stmt-appth-bounds"></a>
<div id="appth:bounds" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15**. *Let $\lambda_0$ be the positive leading eigenvalue of $X_{b_0}$, and let $c_0$ be the squared norm of the projection of $v_{b_0}$ onto its eigenspace. For $$P_*(x)=x^3-.14x^2-.18x,$$ one has <a id="appth:conclusions"></a>$$\label{appth:conclusions}
 Y_6(b_0)<.027,
 \qquad c_0P_*(\lambda_0)^2<.003745.$$*

</div>

The first bound controls the residual contribution to $Y_m(b_0)$ in Lemma <a href="#in:seedbounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="in:seedbounds">10</a>; the second controls the leading contribution. We first reduce both bounds to fifteen short-chain partition functions, then enclose these traces using matrices of order at most $3^6=729$.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Reduction to fifteen thermal traces

For $2\le n\le6$ and $g\in\{1,-1,0\}$, define <a id="appth:H"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 H_{n,g}
 &=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}\mathbf S_j\mathbin\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1}
       -\frac35S_0^z+\frac35R_g,
 &
 R_g&=
 \begin{cases}
    gS_{n-1}^z,&g=1,-1,\\
    S_{n-1}^x,&g=0,
 \end{cases}
 \label{appth:H}\\
 Z_{n,g}&=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+anI)].\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $Z_{n,1}=Y_n(b_0)$ and $Z_{n,-1}=Y_n^{\rm s}(b_0)$. The first bound in Proposition <a href="#appth:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="appth:bounds">15</a> concerns $Z_{6,1}$. For the second, average the right-field direction over the rotation group.

<a id="appth:average"></a>
<a id="appth:polynomial"></a>
Let $\mathcal G$ be the rotation group generated by the diagonal half-turns and the cyclic permutation of the coordinate axes, and let $$\Pi=\frac1{|\mathcal G|}\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal G}U_\gamma,
 \qquad I(n)=\langle v_{b_0},X_{b_0}^n\Pi v_{b_0}\rangle.$$ The group has order $12$, and the orbit of $z$ is the six signed coordinate axes, each with the same multiplicity. The boundary transfer identity therefore writes $I(n)$ as the average of the six corresponding right-field partition functions. Global rotations about $z$ identify the four perpendicular orientations. Hence $$\label{appth:average}
 I(n)=\frac{Z_{n,1}+Z_{n,-1}+4Z_{n,0}}6.$$ The averaging operator $\Pi$ is an orthogonal projection commuting with $X_{b_0}$. By Proposition <a href="#per:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="per:concentration">6</a>, $\mathcal G$ fixes the leading line pointwise. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 c_0P_*(\lambda_0)^2
 &\le\|P_*(X_{b_0})\Pi v_{b_0}\|^2\nonumber\\
 &=\langle v_{b_0},P_*(X_{b_0})^2\Pi v_{b_0}\rangle\nonumber\\
 &=I(6)-.28I(5)-.3404I(4)+.0504I(3)+.0324I(2).
 \label{appth:polynomial}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, no positivity of the individual odd powers of $X_{b_0}$ is being assumed.

It therefore suffices to enclose $Z_{n,g}$ for $2\le n\le6$ and $g\in\{1,-1,0\}$. The remaining subsections provide these enclosures and evaluate <a href="#appth:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:polynomial">[appth:polynomial]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### An integer matrix for the thermal approximation

Set <a id="appth:C"></a>$$\label{appth:C}
 C_n=3\left\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\right\rfloor
          +2\bigl((n-1)\bmod2\bigr)+\frac65,
 \qquad A=I-\frac{H_{n,g}+C_nI}{8}.$$

The operator $A$ is a self-adjoint contraction. Here is a direct verification with the spin normalization in the theorem. A single bond has eigenvalues $-2,-1,1$. For two consecutive bonds, couple the two outside spins to spin $t\in\{0,1,2\}$ and then couple this with the middle spin to total spin $j$. The operator $$\mathbf S_1\mathbin\cdot\mathbf S_2+
 \mathbf S_2\mathbin\cdot\mathbf S_3
   =\mathbf S_2\mathbin\cdot(\mathbf S_1+\mathbf S_3)$$ has eigenvalues $[j(j+1)-t(t+1)-2]/2$, with $|t-1|\le j\le t+1$. Its minimum is $-3$. Pairing consecutive bonds, with one single bond left over if necessary, and using the total boundary-field norm $6/5$ gives $$0\le H_{n,g}+C_nI
    \le\left(n-1+\frac65+C_n\right)I\le16I.$$ Adding the lower bounds for the paired bonds is valid even where the three-site supports overlap. In order $n=2,\ldots,6$, the displayed upper bounds are $5.4,7.4,10.4,12.4,15.4$. Thus $-I\le A\le I$; positivity of $A$ itself is not required.

Index the spin-weight basis by words $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{n-1})$ in $\{-1,0,1\}^n$, and let $$p_w=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}w_j\right)\bmod2\in\{0,1\},
 \qquad J_{ww}=\sqrt{1+p_w},\qquad D=80JAJ^{-1}.$$ The matrix $D$ is integral. Indeed, its diagonal is <a id="appth:diagonal"></a>$$\label{appth:diagonal}
 \eta(w)=80-10C_n-10\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}w_jw_{j+1}
                  +6w_0-6g w_{n-1}.$$ Each allowed bond hop $w\leftrightarrow
w+\sigma(\mathbf e_j-\mathbf e_{j+1})$, $\sigma=\pm1$, has entry $-10$: the corresponding entry of the spin-one bond is $1$, and such a hop preserves $p_w$. When $g=0$, an allowed end hop from column $r=w+\sigma\mathbf e_{n-1}$ to row $w$ has entry $-3(1+p_w)$. To see this coefficient, the end-field entry before similarity is $3\sqrt2/10$, and the two parities are opposite, so $$-10\frac{3\sqrt2}{10}
       \sqrt{\frac{1+p_w}{1+p_r}}=-3(1+p_w).$$ These are all the entries of $D$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The exact recurrence

The recurrence approximates the half-temperature exponential $$E=\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+C_nI)/2]=e^{-49}\exp(49A).$$ For a finite matrix $M$, write $\|M\|_{\rm F}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(M^*M)$ for its squared Frobenius norm. Since $E$ is self-adjoint, $\|E\|_{\rm F}^2$ is the thermal trace $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+C_nI)]$. We approximate $E$ by a normalized Poisson polynomial in the contraction $A$; the similarity $D=80JAJ^{-1}$ permits an integer Horner recurrence.

<a id="appth:coefficients"></a>
Put $$\label{appth:coefficients}
 K=192,\qquad Q=2^{50},\qquad
 s_i=\frac{49^iK!}{i!},\qquad S_K=\sum_{i=0}^Ks_i,
 \qquad t_i=\left\lfloor\frac{Qs_i}{S_K}\right\rfloor.$$ Starting with the zero matrix $\mathcal X_{K+1}$, compute <a id="appth:horner"></a>$$\label{appth:horner}
 \mathcal X_i=
       \left\lfloor\frac{D\mathcal X_{i+1}}{80}\right\rfloor+t_iI,
 \qquad i=K,K-1,\ldots,0.$$ Matrix floors are entrywise, and every floor, including one with a negative argument, is toward $-\infty$. Define <a id="appth:W"></a>$$\label{appth:W}
 \widehat E=J^{-1}\frac{\mathcal X_0}{Q}J,
 \qquad W_{n,g}=\|\widehat E\|_{\rm F}^2.$$

<a id="appth:scalar"></a>
<a id="appth:quotient"></a>
For completeness, the following scalar version specifies the recurrence without matrix construction. Number words, if desired, by $\sum_j(w_j+1)3^j$, and interpret a lookup outside $\{-1,0,1\}^n$ as zero. Initialize $x(w,r)=0$ for all pairs of words. At stage $i=K,\ldots,0$, use the current array to form $$\begin{aligned}
 z(w,r)={}&\eta(w)x(w,r)
       -10\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\sigma=\pm1}
             x\bigl(w+\sigma(\mathbf e_j-\mathbf e_{j+1}),r\bigr)
       \nonumber\\
 &\hspace{5mm}
       -\mathbf1_{\{g=0\}}\,3(1+p_w)
             \sum_{\sigma=\pm1}x(w+\sigma\mathbf e_{n-1},r),
       \label{appth:scalar}\\
 x_{\rm new}(w,r)={}&t_i\mathbf1_{\{w=r\}}
                    +\left\lfloor\frac{z(w,r)}{80}\right\rfloor.
       \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Replace all entries simultaneously. At the end, the required table entry is the integer quotient $$\label{appth:quotient}
 k_{n,g}:=\left\lfloor10^{16}W_{n,g}\right\rfloor
 =\left\lfloor
 \frac{10^{16}\displaystyle\sum_{w,r}
                 x(w,r)^2(2-p_w)(1+p_r)}{2Q^2}
 \right\rfloor.$$ The weight follows from $(1+p_r)/(1+p_w)=(2-p_w)(1+p_r)/2$. All operations in <a href="#appth:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:coefficients">[appth:coefficients]</a>, <a href="#appth:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:scalar">[appth:scalar]</a>, and <a href="#appth:quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:quotient">[appth:quotient]</a> are integer operations; they may be performed with arbitrary-precision integers.

<a id="appth:integer-table"></a>
The resulting fifteen entries are $$\label{appth:integer-table}
\begin{array}{c|rrr}
 n\backslash g&1&-1&0\\\hline
 2&10185296075298&4160017987&583578389575\\
 3&259254948723&571111660440989&47796720049308\\
 4&318278534893&661347985&43971049724\\
 5&260908126900&29917222640283&5659378873682\\
 6&16467340956&328219854&4086161507
\end{array}$$ Equations <a href="#appth:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:coefficients">[appth:coefficients]</a>, <a href="#appth:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:scalar">[appth:scalar]</a>, and <a href="#appth:quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:quotient">[appth:quotient]</a> provide a finite exact-arithmetic verification of every entry.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Error of the matrix approximation

<a id="stmt-appth-matrix-error"></a>
<div id="appth:matrix-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *For every pair $(n,g)$ in <a href="#appth:integer-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:integer-table">[appth:integer-table]</a>, $$\left\|\widehat E-\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+C_nI)/2]\right\|_{\rm F}
       <2\cdot10^{-10}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-appth-matrix-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-appth-matrix-error</summary>

<a id="appth:tail"></a>
*Proof.* Write $d=3^n$. Since $b_0/2=49/8$, the exact target is $$E=e^{-49}\exp(49A)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}
                         e^{-49}\frac{49^i}{i!}A^i.$$ The polynomial with coefficients $q_i=s_i/S_K$ is the truncation of this Poisson expansion, renormalized to have coefficient sum one. Its coefficient $\ell^1$ error is twice the omitted Poisson mass, at most $2R$, where $$\label{appth:tail}
R=\frac{49^{193}}{193!}\frac1{1-49/194}<3.152\cdot10^{-33}.$$ In bounding the omitted Poisson mass by $R$, we have discarded the factor $e^{-49}$. This coefficient estimate is used in (Fox and Glynn 1988, proof of Proposition 1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[FoxGlynn1988](#ref-FoxGlynn1988)]; here it is applied to powers of a contraction. Because $A$ is a contraction, the corresponding Frobenius error is at most $2\sqrt d\,R$.

At each Horner stage the coefficient replacement $q_i\mapsto t_i/Q$ contributes less than $\sqrt d/Q$ in Frobenius norm. The entrywise floor contributes less than $d/Q$ before undoing the similarity, and hence less than $\sqrt2\,d/Q$ afterwards, since $\|J^{-1}\|\,\|J\|\le\sqrt2$. Subsequent propagation is left multiplication by $A$ and cannot increase the Frobenius norm. Thus the contraction is used after undoing the similarity; no contraction property of the generally nonsymmetric integer matrix $D$ is asserted. The total error is less than <a id="appth:frobenius-error"></a>$$\label{appth:frobenius-error}
 2\sqrt d\,R+\frac{193(\sqrt d+\sqrt2\,d)}{Q}
 \le54R+\frac{193(27+\sqrt2\,729)}{2^{50}}
 <54R+\frac{432513}{2^{51}}<2\cdot10^{-10}.$$ The penultimate inequality uses only $\sqrt2<3/2$; the last one is a rational comparison using <a href="#appth:tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:tail">[appth:tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="appth:partition-error"></a>
Squaring the Frobenius norm converts this estimate into a thermal trace. In fact, $E$ is self-adjoint and $\|E\|_{\rm F}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp[-b_0(H_{n,g}+C_nI)]$. Consequently $$\label{appth:partition-error}
 \left|Z_{n,g}-e^{b_0(C_n-an)}W_{n,g}\right|
 <e^{b_0(C_n-an)}
        \left(4\cdot10^{-10}\sqrt{W_{n,g}}+4\cdot10^{-20}\right).$$ This follows from $|\|E\|_{\rm F}^2-\|\widehat E\|_{\rm F}^2|
\le2\|E-\widehat E\|_{\rm F}\|\widehat E\|_{\rm F}
     +\|E-\widehat E\|_{\rm F}^2$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Rational intervals from the printed table

<a id="appth:interval-data"></a>
<a id="appth:partition-interval"></a>
<a id="appth:scalar-exp"></a>
<a id="appth:Y6-interval"></a>
The displayed integer floors suffice to verify the needed estimates; no unprinted digits of $W_{n,g}$ are used. For an entry $k=k_{n,g}$, put $$\label{appth:interval-data}
 L=\frac{k}{10^{16}},\qquad U=\frac{k+1}{10^{16}},\qquad
 r=\frac{\lfloor\sqrt{k+1}\rfloor+1}{10^8},\qquad
 \varepsilon=4\cdot10^{-10}r+4\cdot10^{-20}.$$ Then $L\le W_{n,g}<U$ and $\sqrt{W_{n,g}}<r$. In every entry $L-\varepsilon>0$, so <a href="#appth:partition-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:partition-error">[appth:partition-error]</a> gives $$\label{appth:partition-interval}
 e^{b_0(C_n-an)}(L-\varepsilon)
       <Z_{n,g}<e^{b_0(C_n-an)}(U+\varepsilon).$$ The square root in <a href="#appth:interval-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:interval-data">[appth:interval-data]</a> is just an integer square root. To enclose the remaining exponential by rational numbers, set $$T_{80}(y)=\sum_{j=0}^{80}\frac{y^j}{j!}.$$ For $0\le y\le10$, comparison of consecutive tail terms gives $$\label{appth:scalar-exp}
 T_{80}(y)\le e^y
       \le T_{80}(y)+\frac{2y^{81}}{81!}.$$ For a negative argument, take reciprocals and reverse the endpoints. All arguments $b_0(C_n-an)$ here lie between $-.0544635$ and $9.691073$. Thus <a href="#appth:interval-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:interval-data">[appth:interval-data]</a>–<a href="#appth:scalar-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:scalar-exp">[appth:scalar-exp]</a> require only rational arithmetic and integer square roots. In particular they give $$\label{appth:Y6-interval}
 .0266319440<Z_{6,1}<.0266319607<.027.$$

Using <a href="#appth:partition-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:partition-interval">[appth:partition-interval]</a> and <a href="#appth:scalar-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:scalar-exp">[appth:scalar-exp]</a> in <a href="#appth:average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:average">[appth:average]</a> gives the following rational centers, each with absolute error less than $10^{-7}$: <a id="appth:moment-centers"></a>$$\label{appth:moment-centers}
\begin{array}{c|ccccc}
 n&2&3&4&5&6\\\hline
 \text{center of }I(n)
 &.02703074&.01203562&.01193511&.00931560&.00893272.
\end{array}$$

Substitution of these centers in the right-hand side of <a href="#appth:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:polynomial">[appth:polynomial]</a> gives $.003744031780$. The sum of the absolute polynomial coefficients is $1.7032$, so the total possible error is less than $1.7032\cdot10^{-7}$. The right-hand side of <a href="#appth:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:polynomial">[appth:polynomial]</a> is consequently less than $$.003744031780+.00000017032=.0037442021<.003745.$$ Together with <a href="#appth:Y6-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="appth:Y6-interval">[appth:Y6-interval]</a>, this proves Proposition <a href="#appth:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="appth:bounds">15</a>.

<a id="app:trials"></a>
### Exact trial vectors for opposite fields

We prove the four variational inequalities used to initialize the boundary argument. Throughout this appendix, $$a=\frac{700741}{500000},\qquad h=\frac35,\qquad
 \alpha=\frac{2021}{5000}=.4042,$$ and the unshifted opposite-field Hamiltonian on $m$ sites is <a id="apptr:hamiltonian"></a>$$\label{apptr:hamiltonian}
 H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}
 =\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\mathbf S_j\cdot\mathbf S_{j+1}
   -\frac35S_1^z+\frac35S_m^z.$$ The bulk matrices below are those of the periodic variational construction in (OpenAI 2026, Section “Two variational inputs”) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[periodic](#ref-periodic)]. We give their complete integer specification and the new endpoint vectors, so the present finite estimates can be reproduced without importing a variational bound for open chains.

<a id="stmt-apptr-bound"></a>
<div id="apptr:bound" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17**. *Let $E_0^{\mathrm{opp}}(m)$ be the smallest eigenvalue of $H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}$. Then <a id="apptr:four-bounds"></a>$$\label{apptr:four-bounds}
 E_0^{\mathrm{opp}}(m)+am<\alpha
 \qquad\text{for }m\in\{34,78,142,240\}.$$*

</div>

The proof consists of a fixed integer matrix-product vector, an exact contraction for its norm and energy, and four integer comparisons. No spectral approximation enters the calculation.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The complete tensor and endpoint data

<a id="apptr:virtual-labels"></a>
Let $\mathcal V$ have the $26$ labels $$\label{apptr:virtual-labels}
 (i,d),\qquad 1\le i\le8,\qquad
 d=-\ell_i,-\ell_i+2,\ldots,\ell_i,
 \qquad(\ell_i)_{i=1}^8=(1,1,1,1,3,3,3,5).$$ Order them first by $i$ and then by increasing $d$. Three real $26$-by-$26$ matrices $A_s$, $s\in\{-1,0,1\}$, are specified as follows. At row $(i,d)$ and column $(j,e)$ put $\ell=\ell_i$, $k=\ell_j$, and $r=(\ell+d)/2$. The entry is zero unless <a id="apptr:support"></a>$$\label{apptr:support}
 e=d+2s,\qquad |k-\ell|\le2,\qquad
 k=\ell\ \Longrightarrow\ i=j.$$ For an allowed entry, multiply the coefficient in the second column of Table [apptr:entry-table] [apptr:entry-table](#apptr:entry-table) by the factor in its $s$ column. The parameters $D_i$ and $P_{ij}$ appear in Tables [apptr:endpoint-table] [apptr:endpoint-table](#apptr:endpoint-table) and <a href="#apptr:coupling-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:coupling-table">3</a>. The latter parameter is defined only when $\ell_j=\ell_i+2$. All labels in these rules must belong to <a href="#apptr:virtual-labels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:virtual-labels">[apptr:virtual-labels]</a>; an out-of-range column is absent.

Define columns $\mathbf b,\mathbf c\in\mathbb R^{26}$ by <a id="apptr:endpoints"></a>$$\label{apptr:endpoints}
 \mathbf b_{(i,d)}=\begin{cases}\beta_i,&d=-1,\\0,&d\ne-1,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 \mathbf c_{(i,d)}=\begin{cases}\kappa_i\beta_i,&d=-1,\\0,&d\ne-1.\end{cases}$$ All their parameters are included in Table [apptr:endpoint-table] [apptr:endpoint-table](#apptr:endpoint-table).

<a id="apptr:physical-vector"></a>
For each $m\ge2$, the physical vector is $$\label{apptr:physical-vector}
 \psi_m=\sum_{s_1,\ldots,s_m=-1}^{1}
 \bigl(\mathbf b^{\mathsf t}A_{s_1}\cdots A_{s_m}\mathbf c\bigr)
 e_{s_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{s_m}.$$ This is the open-boundary matrix-product form of (Pérez-García et al. 2007, sec. 3.1, Equation (11)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[PerezGarcia2007](#ref-PerezGarcia2007)], with endpoint rows and columns absorbed into the first and last matrices. The vectors $e_{-1},e_0,e_1$ are the orthonormal spin basis fixed in the statement of the problem. In particular, the coefficients in <a href="#apptr:physical-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:physical-vector">[apptr:physical-vector]</a> are ordinary real amplitudes: no virtual inner product or physical basis weights are inserted. Normalization of the matrices $A_s$ is unnecessary. The support rule forces a nonzero amplitude to have $\sum_j s_j=0$, because both endpoints have virtual weight $-1$. This restriction on the trial vector does not restrict the variational upper bound on the smallest eigenvalue of the full Hamiltonian.

The following tables complete the integer specification of these matrices and endpoints.

<div id="apptr:coupling-table">

|            | coefficient |         $s=-1$          |       $s=0$        |    $s=1$     |
|:----------:|:-----------:|:-----------------------:|:------------------:|:------------:|
| $k=\ell+2$ |  $P_{ij}$   | $2(\ell+1-r)(\ell+2-r)$ | $2(r+1)(\ell-r+1)$ | $(r+1)(r+2)$ |
|  $k=\ell$  |    $D_i$    |      $2(\ell-r+1)$      |        $d$         |   $-(r+1)$   |
| $k=\ell-2$ |  $-P_{ji}$  |           $2$           |        $-2$        |     $1$      |

All fifteen couplings with $\ell_j=\ell_i+2$.

</div>

<div id="apptr:coupling-table-2">

| $i$ | $\ell_i$ |  $D_i$ | $\beta_i$ | $\kappa_i$ |
|----:|---------:|-------:|----------:|-----------:|
|   1 |        1 | $-289$ |     10000 |         12 |
|   2 |        1 |  $-84$ |      5620 |         12 |
|   3 |        1 |   $-9$ |      2456 |         12 |
|   4 |        1 |     33 |      4868 |         12 |
|   5 |        3 |  $-22$ |        82 |          6 |
|   6 |        3 |     27 |    $-809$ |          6 |
|   7 |        3 |     71 |     12475 |          6 |
|   8 |        5 |  $-11$ |   $-9645$ |          1 |

All fifteen couplings with $\ell_j=\ell_i+2$.

</div>

<div id="apptr:coupling-table-3">

| $(i,j)$ | $P_{ij}$ | $(i,j)$ | $P_{ij}$ | $(i,j)$ | $P_{ij}$ |
|:-------:|---------:|:-------:|---------:|:-------:|---------:|
| $(1,5)$ |    $-11$ | $(2,7)$ |    $-49$ | $(4,6)$ |        9 |
| $(1,6)$ |    $-35$ | $(3,5)$ |     $-6$ | $(4,7)$ |    $-32$ |
| $(1,7)$ |    $-28$ | $(3,6)$ |    $-25$ | $(5,8)$ |        1 |
| $(2,5)$ |    $-23$ | $(3,7)$ |    $-20$ | $(6,8)$ |        2 |
| $(2,6)$ |       16 | $(4,5)$ |    $-34$ | $(7,8)$ |    $-16$ |

All fifteen couplings with $\ell_j=\ell_i+2$.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Norm, fields, and the bond insertion

<a id="apptr:bond-insertion"></a>
For $s,t\in\{-1,0,1\}$, put $B_{st}=A_sA_t$ and define <a id="apptr:one-site-transfers"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 T_*&=\sum_{s=-1}^{1}A_s\otimes A_s,
 &G_*&=\sum_{s=-1}^{1}sA_s\otimes A_s,
 \label{apptr:one-site-transfers}\\
 O_*&=\sum_{s,t=-1}^{1}st\,B_{st}\otimes B_{st}
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\quad+
 \sum_{\substack{s=-1,0\\t=0,1}}
 \bigl(B_{st}\otimes B_{s+1,t-1}
       +B_{s+1,t-1}\otimes B_{st}\bigr).
 \label{apptr:bond-insertion}
\end{aligned}$$ We use the usual Kronecker convention $(C\otimes D)_{(v,w),(v',w')}=C_{v,v'}D_{w,w'}$. Write <a id="apptr:boundary-tensors"></a>$$\label{apptr:boundary-tensors}
 L_0=(\mathbf b\otimes\mathbf b)^{\mathsf t},
 \qquad u=\mathbf c\otimes\mathbf c,
 \qquad L_i=L_0T_*^i.$$

<a id="stmt-apptr-contractions"></a>
<div id="apptr:contractions" class="lemma">

<a id="apptr:norm"></a>
<a id="apptr:energy-expanded"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *With the data above, the norm $V_m=\langle\psi_m,\psi_m\rangle$ and the scaled shifted energy numerator $$H_m^*=500000\,
 \langle\psi_m,(H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}+amI)\psi_m\rangle$$ satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 V_m&=L_mu,\label{apptr:norm}\\
 H_m^*&=500000\sum_{r=0}^{m-2}
          L_0T_*^rO_*T_*^{m-2-r}u
       -300000L_0G_*T_*^{m-1}u\notag\\
 &\quad+300000L_{m-1}G_*u+700741mL_mu.
 \label{apptr:energy-expanded}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $V_m$ and $H_m^*$ are integers.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-apptr-contractions"><summary>Proof of stmt-apptr-contractions</summary>

*Proof.* Expanding $T_*^m$ sums the products of two identical real amplitudes over all physical words. This proves <a href="#apptr:norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:norm">[apptr:norm]</a> directly in the orthonormal physical basis. Inserting $G_*$ at one site instead of $T_*$ multiplies the summand by the weight $s$ and therefore inserts $S^z$ at that site.

For a bond, the spin normalization gives $$\mathbf S\cdot\mathbf S
 =S^z\otimes S^z+\tfrac12
   (S^+\otimes S^-+S^-\otimes S^+).$$ Its diagonal entry on $(s,t)$ is $st$. Its off-diagonal entries are $1$ between $(s,t)$ and $(s+1,t-1)$ for $s\in\{-1,0\}$ and $t\in\{0,1\}$, since each allowed ladder entry is $\sqrt2$. The two summands in each parenthesis of <a href="#apptr:bond-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:bond-insertion">[apptr:bond-insertion]</a> give the two directions of that matrix element. Thus $O_*$ inserts exactly one unit-coupling bond, with no extra factor or metric. Its position after $r$ sites gives the $r$th summand of <a href="#apptr:energy-expanded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:energy-expanded">[apptr:energy-expanded]</a>.

The left and right endpoint insertions have coefficients $500000(-3/5)=-300000$ and $500000(3/5)=300000$, respectively. The constant shift contributes $500000am=700741m$ times the norm. There are $m-1$ bond positions and no closing bond, giving the stated formula. Every matrix, row, and column in that formula is integral. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The invariant 162-dimensional contraction

All contractions in Lemma <a href="#apptr:contractions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:contractions">18</a> can be carried out in a single $162$-dimensional space. On a paired virtual label $((i,d),(j,e))$, consider the difference $d-e$. Each nonzero entry of $A_s$ changes virtual weight by $2s$ from row to column. Consequently every summand $A_s\otimes A_s$ of $T_*$ or $G_*$ preserves this difference. A nonzero entry of $B_{st}$ changes weight by $2(s+t)$. The two factors in every tensor summand of $O_*$ have the same total physical weight, including the pair $(s,t)$ and $(s+1,t-1)$ in the off-diagonal terms. Each such summand also preserves $d-e$.

<a id="apptr:restricted-labels"></a>
Let $\mathcal K_0$ be the coordinate subspace with paired labels $$\label{apptr:restricted-labels}
 \mathcal Q=\{((i,d),(j,d)):(i,d),(j,d)\in\mathcal V\}.$$ The multiplicities of the virtual weights $d=-5,-3,-1,1,3,5$ are $1,4,8,8,4,1$, respectively; hence <a id="apptr:restricted-dimension"></a>$$\label{apptr:restricted-dimension}
 \dim\mathcal K_0=1^2+4^2+8^2+8^2+4^2+1^2=162.$$ The preceding argument shows that $T_*,G_*,O_*$ are block diagonal by weight difference. Both $L_0$ and $u$ are supported in $\mathcal K_0$, because the two endpoint vectors are supported on $d=-1$. We may therefore restrict these three matrices to <a href="#apptr:restricted-labels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:restricted-labels">[apptr:restricted-labels]</a> before multiplying them, taking powers, or forming the expressions of Lemma <a href="#apptr:contractions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:contractions">18</a>. In particular, squaring the restricted $T_*$ gives the restriction of $T_*^2$; there is no contribution through a discarded state. From now on the same symbols denote these restricted integer matrices and endpoint tensors, ordered by the inherited lexicographic order.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### An exact row recurrence

<a id="apptr:recurrence"></a>
<a id="apptr:energy-recurrence"></a>
The energy sum in <a href="#apptr:energy-expanded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:energy-expanded">[apptr:energy-expanded]</a> need not be evaluated separately at every bond. Define integer rows by $$\begin{aligned}
 L_i&=L_{i-1}T_*\quad(i\ge1),\notag\\
 N_1&=-300000L_0G_*,\notag\\
 N_i&=N_{i-1}T_*+
       L_{i-2}\bigl(500000O_*+700741T_*^2\bigr)
       \quad(i\ge2).
 \label{apptr:recurrence}
\end{aligned}$$ Then $$\label{apptr:energy-recurrence}
 V_m=L_mu,\qquad
 H_m^*=\bigl(N_m+300000L_{m-1}G_*+700741L_m\bigr)u.$$ To verify the latter identity, induction on $m$ in <a href="#apptr:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:recurrence">[apptr:recurrence]</a> gives <a id="apptr:N-expansion"></a>$$\label{apptr:N-expansion}
 N_m=-300000L_0G_*T_*^{m-1}
   +\sum_{r=0}^{m-2}
      L_0T_*^r(500000O_*+700741T_*^2)T_*^{m-2-r}.$$ Its bond contributions and left field are exactly those in <a href="#apptr:energy-expanded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:energy-expanded">[apptr:energy-expanded]</a>. Each of its $m-1$ shift contributions equals $700741L_m$. The final term $700741L_m$ in <a href="#apptr:energy-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:energy-recurrence">[apptr:energy-recurrence]</a> supplies the remaining site shift, and the other final term supplies the right field with the required positive sign. This proves <a href="#apptr:energy-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:energy-recurrence">[apptr:energy-recurrence]</a>.

<a id="apptr:algorithm"></a>
<a id="apptr:readout"></a>
For specificity, the whole contraction can be implemented by keeping just three rows. Begin with $$x=L_0,\qquad y=L_0T_*,\qquad w=-300000L_0G_*.$$ For each $m=2,3,\ldots,240$, make the following updates, whose right-hand sides all use the old rows: $$\begin{aligned}
 w_{\mathrm{new}}&=wT_*+500000xO_*+700741(xT_*)T_*,\notag\\
 x_{\mathrm{new}}&=y,\qquad y_{\mathrm{new}}=yT_*.
 \label{apptr:algorithm}
\end{aligned}$$ After this iteration, $(x,y,w)=(L_{m-1},L_m,N_m)$. At a required length compute $$\label{apptr:readout}
 V=yu,\qquad H=wu+300000x(G_*u)+700741V.$$ Thus $(V,H)=(V_m,H_m^*)$. The initialization realizes the same invariant at $m=1$, which proves the indexing of the algorithm. Ordinary exact integer matrix multiplication suffices throughout; sparsity can reduce the work but changes no operation or comparison.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The integer comparisons

<a id="apptr:certificate-inequalities"></a>
Table <a href="#apptr:certificate-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:certificate-table">4</a> gives the results of <a href="#apptr:algorithm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:algorithm">[apptr:algorithm]</a>–<a href="#apptr:readout" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:readout">[apptr:readout]</a>. The integers $p_m$, $q_m$, and $k_m$ in the table have the following exact meanings: $$\begin{aligned}
 10^{p_m-1}&\le V_m<10^{p_m},\notag\\
 q_m10^{p_m-6}&\le V_m<(q_m+1)10^{p_m-6},\notag\\
 k_mV_m&\le20H_m^*<(k_m+1)V_m.
 \label{apptr:certificate-inequalities}
\end{aligned}$$ The last comparison is equivalent to $k_m=\lfloor10^7H_m^*/(500000V_m)\rfloor$, since $10^7/500000=20$. The first comparison in particular certifies that the vector $\psi_m$ is nonzero.

<div id="apptr:certificate-table">

| $m$ | $p_m$ |  $q_m$ |   $k_m$ | $H_m^*/(500000V_m)$ lies in |
|----:|------:|-------:|--------:|:---------------------------:|
|  34 |   202 | 715867 | 4031974 |    $[.4031974,.4031975)$    |
|  78 |   440 | 747265 | 4041175 |    $[.4041175,.4041176)$    |
| 142 |   787 | 121288 | 4041110 |    $[.4041110,.4041111)$    |
| 240 |  1317 | 165352 | 4041006 |    $[.4041006,.4041007)$    |

Exact integer contraction certificates. The intervals are rational intervals obtained from integer division, with strict upper endpoints.

</div>

<a id="apptr:strict-margin"></a>
All four strict comparisons $$\label{apptr:strict-margin}
 202100V_m-H_m^*>0
 \qquad(m=34,78,142,240)$$ follow as well. They are recorded directly in the full integer output, and they also follow from the last line of <a href="#apptr:certificate-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:certificate-inequalities">[apptr:certificate-inequalities]</a>, since every displayed $k_m+1$ is less than $4042000=20\cdot202100$.

The accompanying computational files make the integer comparison fully reproducible. The program constructs the matrices from the data in this appendix and executes the row recurrence with arbitrary-precision integers. The file contains every entry of $A_s$, the endpoint vectors, the restricted paired labels, and the matrices $T_*,G_*,O_*$. The file contains the full integers $V_m$, $H_m^*$, and $202100V_m-H_m^*$ for each row, rather than only their shortened display in Table <a href="#apptr:certificate-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:certificate-table">4</a>. The equations and tables of this appendix specify the calculation independently of these files.

<details id="proof-stmt-apptr-contractions-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-apptr-contractions</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#apptr:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:bound">17</a>.* For each of the four lengths, the integer $V_m=\langle\psi_m,\psi_m\rangle$ is positive by <a href="#apptr:certificate-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:certificate-inequalities">[apptr:certificate-inequalities]</a>. The variational principle, Lemma <a href="#apptr:contractions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="apptr:contractions">18</a>, and <a href="#apptr:strict-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="apptr:strict-margin">[apptr:strict-margin]</a> give $$E_0^{\mathrm{opp}}(m)+am
 \le\frac{\langle\psi_m,(H_m^{\mathrm{opp}}+amI)\psi_m\rangle}
          {\langle\psi_m,\psi_m\rangle}
 =\frac{H_m^*}{500000V_m}
 <\frac{202100}{500000}
 =\alpha.$$ This proves all four inequalities with the same fixed field $h=3/5$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two spatial boundary pairings. Each box contributes one site kernel; the internal integrations represent the $n − 1$ bonds. Replacing the right boundary vector by $U_{P}v_{b}$ changes the right physical field. This is an identity between transfer representations, not a claim that the two physical Hamiltonians are unitarily equivalent.",
      "figure_id": "fig:boundary-pairings",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:boundary-pairings",
          "exact_quote": "The two spatial boundary pairings. Each box contributes one\nsite kernel; the internal integrations represent the $n-1$ bonds.\nReplacing the right boundary vector by $U_Pv_b$ changes the right\nphysical field. This is an identity between tran",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,\n  site/.style={draw,minimum width=9mm,minimum height=8mm},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\foreach \\y in {0,-1.8} {\n   \\node[site] at (0,\\y) {$X_b$};\n   \\node[site] at (1.6,\\y) {$X_b$};\n   \\node at (3.2,\\y) {$\\cdots$};\n   \\node[site] at (4.8,\\y) {$X_b$};\n   \\draw (-1.6,\\y)--(-.45,\\y);\n   \\draw (.45,\\y)--(1.15,\\y);\n   \\draw (2.05,\\y)--(2.75,\\y);\n   \\draw (3.65,\\y)--(4.35,\\y);\n   \\draw (5.25,\\y)--(6.4,\\y);\n   \\node[left] at (-1.6,\\y) {$\\overline v_b$};\n }\n \\node[right] at (6.4,0) {$v_b$};\n \\node[right] at (6.4,-1.8) {$U_Pv_b$};\n \\node[above] at (-.2,.45) {left: $-hS^z$};\n \\node[above] at (5.0,.45) {right: $+hS^z$};\n \\node[below] at (-.2,-2.25) {left: $-hS^z$};\n \\node[below] at (5.0,-2.25) {right: $-hS^z$};\n \\draw[->] (6.7,-.45)--(6.7,-1.35);\n \\node[left] at (6.45,-.9) {$P e_z=-e_z$};\n \\node at (2.4,-.65) {$n$ site kernels};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two horizontal 1D transfer-operator diagrams arranged vertically:\n- Top diagram: starts on the left with vector $\\bar{v}_b$, connected by a horizontal line segment labeled \"left: $-hS^z$\" to a sequence of boxed nodes $X_b$, $X_b$, $\\dots$, $X_b$, and connects on the right by a line segment labeled \"right: $+hS^z$\" to vector $v_b$. A horizontal brace under the sequence of boxes is labeled \"$n\\text{ site kernels}$\".\n- Bottom diagram: starts on the left with vector $\\bar{v}_b$, connected by a line segment labeled below with \"left: $-hS^z$\" to a sequence of boxed nodes $X_b$, $X_b$, $\\dots$, $X_b$, and connects on the right by a line segment labeled below with \"right: $-hS^z$\" to vector $U_P v_b$.\n- Between the two diagrams on the right, a vertical downward arrow connects vector $v_b$ in the top row to vector $U_P v_b$ in the bottom row, labeled to its left with \"$P e_z = -e_z$\"."
    }
  ],
